'''Portugal''',{{efn|{{IPA|PT-pt|puɾtuˈɣal|audio=Portugal.wav|help=no}}}} officially the '''Portuguese Republic''',{{efn|{{langx|PT-pt|República Portuguesa|links=no}}, {{IPA|PT-pt|rɛˈpuβlikɐ puɾtuˈɣez̻ɐ|IPA|help=no}}}} is a country in [[Southwestern Europe]]. [[Continental Portugal|Mainland Portugal]] is located on the southwestern portion of the [[Iberian Peninsula]], and [[Portugal–Spain border|bordered]] by Spain to the north and east. Portugal also includes the archipelagos of [[Madeira]] and the [[Azores]] in the Atlantic Ocean. The country has a population of 11.4 million, and [[Lisbon]], its capital, is the largest city. Portugal's internal waters and territorial sea together account for two-fifths of its territory, and [[Exclusive economic zone of Portugal|its exclusive economic zone]] is one of Europe's largest, while its terrain contains a diverse range of landscapes and regional climates.

The western Iberian Peninsula has been inhabited since [[Prehistoric Iberia|prehistory]], with the earliest signs of settlement dating to between 5500{{spaces}}BC and 5300{{spaces}}BC. Muslims had occupied the Iberian Peninsula [[Battle of Guadalete|since 711]]. As a result of the {{lang|pt-PT|[[Portugal in the Reconquista|Reconquista]]}} against them, Portugal was established as a county of the Kingdom of León in 868 and [[Manifestis Probatum|formally]] as a kingdom in {{YEAR|1179}}. During the [[Age of Discovery]], the kingdom made several advancements in nautical science and maritime exploration to discover new territories and sea routes, which led to the establishment of the [[Portuguese Empire]]. The kingdom [[5 October 1910 revolution|became a republic]] in {{YEAR|1910}}, before [[28 May 1926 coup d'état|becoming a dictatorship]] in {{YEAR|1926}}, whose [[Carnation Revolution|1974 overthrow]] enabled democracy to be established in {{YEAR|1976}}.

Portugal is a unitary republic and multi-party representative democracy with four separate [[Sovereignty body (Portugal)|sovereignty organs]]: president, government, parliament, and judiciary. It has a unicameral national legislature known as the [[Assembly of the Republic (Portugal)|Assembly of the Republic]] ({{lang|pt|Assembleia da República}}). Portugal is divided into [[NUTS statistical regions of Portugal|seven mainland regions]] and [[Autonomous regions of Portugal|two autonomous overseas regions]], though it remains a highly centralised country.

A [[developed country]], Portugal has an advanced economy that chiefly relies upon services, industry, and tourism. Shaped by the various civilisations that have inhabited its territory, such as the Carthaginians and the Romans Portugal developed a [[Culture of Portugal|culture]] with a worldwide influence that allowed Portuguese to become the world's [[List of languages by number of native speakers|fifth-most spoken native language]] with more than 250 million native speakers. With a stable foreign policy shaped by its colonial and diplomatic history and location, Portugal is a member of [[International organization membership of Portugal|multiple international organisations and forums]].

== Etymology ==

The word {{anchor|Name|Etymology}} ''Portugal'' derives from [[Latin]] {{lang|la|[[Portus Cale]]}}, a [[Ancient Rome|Roman]] name meaning 'port of {{lang|la|Cale}}', itself the origin of the name of the Portuguese city of {{lang|pt|[[Porto]]}}, while ''Cale'' was a town on the [[Douro]]. There is no consensus on the etymology of ''Cale''. It could derive from the [[Pre-Indo-European languages|pre-Indo-European]] word ''Kala'', meaning 'shelter' or 'refuge', passed into the [[Callaecian language|Celtic language]] in the form ''Cale'', with the meaning of 'land' or 'mountain'. The [[ethnonym]] ''Calaico/a'' then came from the [[Gallaeci]], Celtic peoples of northwestern Iberia, and came to mean 'one of the land' or 'one of the place.{{sfnp|Carvalho|2022|pp=76–79}}{{sfnp|Magarinhos|2011|pp=537–546}}{{sfnp|Silva|2010|pp=217-218,235}} During the [[Middle Ages]], the region around Portus Cale became known by the [[Visigoths]] as ''Portucale'' and by the [[Suebi]] as ''Parochiale''.{{sfnp|Silva|2010|pp=213–262}} The name ''Portucale'' evolved into ''Portugale''; by the 11th and 12th centuries ''Portugal'' was referred to the region between the Douro and [[Minho (river)|Minho]] rivers.{{sfnp|Wade|2020|p=25}}

== History ==
{{Main|History of Portugal}}

=== Prehistory ===
{{Main|Prehistoric Iberia}}

The region has been inhabited by humans since approximately 400,000 years ago.{{efn|The earliest human trace found in Portugal is the 400,000-year-old [[Aroeira 3]], a {{lang|la|[[Homo heidelbergensis]]}} skull discovered in the [[Cave of Aroeira]] in 2014.{{sfnp|Daura|Sanz|Arsuaga|Hoffmann|2017|pp=3397–3402}}}} [[Neanderthal]]s roamed the southwestern Iberian Peninsula until 37,000 years ago,{{sfnp|Zilhão|Cardoso|Pike|Weninger|2011|pp=93–112}} after which {{lang|la|[[Homo sapiens sapiens]]}} arrived in what is now Portugal around 35,000 years ago and spread rapidly.{{sfnp|Disney|2009|p=5}} The onset of the [[Neolithic Europe|Neolithic]] in the area dates to between 5500{{spaces}}BC and 5300{{spaces}}BC.{{sfnp|Peyroteo-Stjerna|2021|pp=265–299}} In southern Portugal, [[Iron Age Europe|Iron Age]] inscribed [[stele]] have been found, representing an early evidence of writing in the Iberian Peninsula.{{sfnp|Luján Martínez|2020|pp=561–589}} Pre-Celtic tribes originally from the [[Steppe theory|Ukrainian steppes]] inhabited mainland Portugal and the [[Lusitanians]] occupied mostly central, inland regions in 1250{{spaces}}BC.{{sfnp|Gorbea|2023|pp=251-254}}{{sfnp|Alarcão|2001|pp=322-324}} [[Celts|Celtic]] may have spread westwards in Iberia in the latter 1st millennium BC.{{sfnp|Sims-Williams|2020|p=523}} [[Phoenecia]]ns colonised Portugal during the 7th and 8th centuries BC.{{sfnp|Prieto|2026|p=[https://books.google.pt/books?id=6_qaEQAAQBAJ&lpg=PA18 18]}}

=== Antiquity and early Middle Ages ===
{{Main|Lusitania|Gallaecia|Hispania|Ancient Portugal}}
[[File:Roman Temple Evora Portugal 02.jpg|thumb|upright 0.90|The [[Roman Temple of Évora]], built in the 1st century|alt=Ruins of the Roman Temple of Évora with tall Corinthian columns standing on a raised platform made of rocks in a sunlit town square, set against a bright blue sky, with a medieval tower and white buildings in the background.]]

The Mediterranean coast was well known to the [[Ancient Carthage|Carthaginians]], whose close relations with Phoenician-Punic settlements on shared cultural ties likely gave them considerable political and economic influence there from the 6th or 5th century BC alongside the establishment of unequal treaties with Iberian city-states. After Carthage's defeat by Rome in the [[First Punic War]], it sought new western territories, culminating in the [[Barcid conquest of Hispania|Barcid conquest of the Iberian Peninsula]] in 237{{spaces}}BC.{{sfnp|Ferrer Albelda|Pliego Vázquez|2021|pp=21-22}}{{sfnp|Cutillas-Victoria|Camino|Asensio|2024|p=1}}

Romans [[Second Punic War|invaded the Iberian Peninsula]] in 218{{spaces}}BC and expelled the Carthaginians after the [[Battle of Ilipa]] in 206{{spaces}}BC.{{sfnp|Hoyos|2015|pp=165–167}}{{sfnp|Zimmermann|2011|pp=280-284,290-291,293}} Within 200 years, mainland Portugal had been [[Roman conquest of the Iberian Peninsula|annexed by the Romans]] despite resistance from local tribes such as the [[Lusitanian Wars|Lusitanians]] under the leadership of [[Viriathus]] and other leaders.{{sfnp|Pires Pereira|2024|pp=405-456}}{{sfnp|Fernández-Götz|Maschek|Roymans|2020|pp=1633–1635}}{{sfnp|Gil González|2017|pp=113-138}}

In 409, with the decline of the [[Western Roman Empire]], the Iberian Peninsula was invaded by [[Germanic tribes]].{{sfnp|Disney|2009|pp=34–37,41}} Western Iberia was integrated into the [[Suebian Kingdom]], with its capital in or near [[Braga]].{{sfnp|Sánchez|2015|pp=176–209}}{{sfnp|Disney|2009|p=37}} The Visigoths defeated the Suebi  in 585 and ruled the peninsula until the early 8th century.{{sfnp|Lenski|2022|pp=251–253}}{{sfnp|Collins|2004|pp=60-61}}

In 711, the Iberian Peninsula was [[Muslim conquest of the Iberian Peninsula|invaded from the south]] by the [[Umayyad Caliphate]], which expanded rapidly and by 716 the lands what now form mainland Portugal were part of Muslim Iberian territories known as [[al-Andalus]].{{sfnp|Rusin|2025|pp=7-17}} The Umayyads ruled al-Andalus until it fragmented into small states called [[taifa]]s, with the caliphate being collapsed in 1031,{{sfnp|Rusin|2025|pp=7-17}} and most of what is now central and southern Portugal falling under the [[Taifa of Badajoz]].{{sfnp|Disney||2009|p=56}} The taifas were conquered by Muslim conquerors from North Africa, first completely by the [[Almoravids]] by 1115, who were in turn followed by the [[Almohads]] in the 1140s.{{sfnp|Disney|2009|pp=57,64,78-79}}{{sfnp|Bennison|2016|pp=47,59-61}} Northern incursions also occurred during this period, with [[Viking]] raids recorded from 844 into the 11th century.{{sfnp|Pires|2022|pp=92-95}}

=== ''Reconquista'' and independence ===
{{Main|Portugal in the Reconquista|Portugal in the Middle Ages}}

[[File:Estatua de Vimara Peres, Oporto, Portugal, 2012-05-09, DD 01.JPG|thumb|left|upright=.57|[[Vímara Peres]]|alt=Statue of Vímara Peres]]

The {{lang|pt|[[Reconquista]]}} was a series of military and cultural campaigns by northern Iberian Christian polities against al-Andalus.{{sfnp|Caraccioli|2021|p=15}} The death of the [[Umayyad state of Córdoba|Umayyad]] emir [[Abd ar-Rahman II]] in 852 brought an end to Umayyad efforts to extend authority northward.{{sfnp|Lay|2009|p=7}} The discovery in the 830s of a tomb believed to be that of [[James the Great]] gave the Christians a powerful patron, and the elevation of [[Alfonso III of Asturias]] in the 860s ended a period of uncertain leadership, thereby providing the command required to channel this energy into a wave of expansionism southward.{{sfnp|Lay|2009|p=7}} In 868, the region between the Minho and Douro rivers was secured from the [[Moors]] by Christian forces under [[Vímara Peres]] and constituted as the [[County of Portugal]] under him.{{sfnp|Lay|2009|p=7}} The first county of Portugal lasted until it was abolished following the [[Battle of Pedroso]], in 1071.{{sfnp|Lay|2009|pp=11-13}} In 1096, [[Alfonso VI of León]] refounded the county and bestowed it on [[Henry, Count of Portugal|Henry of Burgundy]], who married Alfonso's [[Legitimacy (family law)|illegitimate]] daughter, [[Theresa, Countess of Portugal|Teresa of León]].{{sfnp|Lay|2009|pp=37-42}}

[[File:D. Afonso Henriques - Compendio de crónicas de reyes (Biblioteca Nacional de España).png|thumb|upright 0.6|King [[Afonso I of Portugal|Afonso I]]|alt=Medieval miniature of King Afonso I]]

After Count Henry's death in 1112, Teresa ruled the county as regent for her son [[Afonso Henriques]].{{sfnp|Godoy da Costa Leal Ferreira|2023|pp=24-37}} Her close association from 1121 with [[Fernão Peres de Trava]], a Galician noble, displeased the local nobility, who sided with Afonso Henriques and defeated her and her supporters at the [[Battle of São Mamede]] in 1128.{{sfnp|Godoy da Costa Leal Ferreira|2023|pp=24-37}}{{sfnp|Lay|2009|p=67}}{{sfnp|Ribeiro Mirada|2025|pp=65-72}} The following year, Afonso claimed sole authority over the entire county, free from foreign influence.{{sfnp|Lay|2009|pp=71-72}} According to legend, he won the [[Battle of Ourique]] and took the title of [[List of Portuguese monarchs|King]] in 1139.{{sfnp|Mendes|2024|pp=147-172}}{{sfnp|Franco da Costa|2014}}{{efn|There are five surviving 12th-century documents that refer to the Battle of Ourique, none of which mention any apparition of Christ to D. Afonso Henriques, the earliest such references appear in the 15th century. According to the historian Rodrigo Franco da Costa: "No document until the 15th century mentions the apparition of Christ to D. Afonso Henriques on the eve of the Battle of Ourique. Those that have been presented are not conclusive. There are, however, 'five' 12th century documents that refer to the battle — yet none of them mention the apparition, even though some narrate miracles. The first certain references to the miracle appear in the mid-15th century. Once it emerged, the legend gradually expanded until it acquired its definitive form in the writers of the sixteenth century ... We therefore have, on one side, the silence of the three centuries closest to the battle concerning the miracle of the apparition, and on the other, the late appearance of the legend under circumstances that point to an evolutionary process. It should also be noted that, according to the 16th century version, Christ did not merely appear and promise victory — He also prophesied the future destinies of Portugal. Thus, the contemporaries of the conquering king supposedly received from God Himself the titles legitimizing the new monarchy and the guarantee of its glorious future. Yet it is precisely this fact of the divine foundation of Portugal — the most important fact of all for the Portuguese — about which everyone remains silent, while they do speak of the battle that supposedly occurred."{{sfnp|Franco da Costa|2014}}}} Afonso's claim was implicitly recognized by [[Alfonso VII of León]] at the [[Conference of Zamora]] in 1143, and by [[Pope Alexander III]] in 1179 through the [[papal bull]] {{lang|la|[[Manifestis Probatum]]}}.{{sfnp|Ferreira|2013}}{{sfnp|Reilly|1998|pp=[https://books.google.pt/books?id=AVIrEAAAQBAJ&pg=PA80 80-81]}}{{sfnp|Veneziani|2024|pp=165-186}}{{efn|Unlike popular belief, there are no surviving documents indicating that a treaty was signed at Zamora in 1143 recognizing Portugal's status as a kingdom by the [[Kingdom of León]]. Historian Bernard F. Reilly states that "In October he invited Afonso Henriques to a conference at Zamora. The Leonese monarch wished not only to secure his own border with Portugal in the coming months but also to obtain the collaboration of his cousin in simultaneous attacks on the Almoravids and he was willing to make the greatest concessions to achieve such cooperation. The evidence for what transpired between the two at Zamora is certainly less than we should wish. In the first instance, it consists in two royal charters, one to a Martin Cidez and another to Pons de Cabrera (D458-59). Although the Portuguese ruler does not appear as a confirmant of either he is cited in the dating formula of both. They speak of the Council of Valladolid celebrated by Cardinal Guido and of the 'king of Portugal' who came to hold a conference with the emperor. Since the charters were drafted by Alfonso VII's chancery, the recognition of the new royal status of Afonso Henriques is implicit."{{sfnp|Reilly|1998|pp=[https://books.google.pt/books?id=AVIrEAAAQBAJ&pg=PA80 80-81]}}}}

With the support of north-European [[Iberian Crusades|crusaders]] and Christian [[Military order (religious society)|military orders]], Afonso Henriques and his successors continued pushing south until the [[Portuguese conquest of the Algarve|capture of Algarve]], the southernmost region of mainland Portugal, which was [[Treaty of Badajoz (1267)|recognised]] by the [[Kingdom of Castile]] as Portuguese territory in 1267.{{sfnp|Wilson|2020|pp=199-229}}{{sfnp|Mendonça|1991|pp=1123-1134}} With minor readjustments, Portugal's borders have largely remained the same since the signing of the [[Treaty of Alcañices]] between [[Denis of Portugal]] and [[Fernando IV of Castile]] in 1297.{{sfnp|García|2006|pp=713–723}} Between the 14th and early 15th century, Portugal was struck by [[Second plague pandemic|several outbreaks of the plague]], civil wars, invasions, famines, and natural disasters that led to a population decrease, labour shortages, and rise in [[Medieval antisemitism|antisemitism]].{{sfnp|Disney|2009|pp=107-109}}

=== Age of Discovery ===
{{main||Portuguese Empire|History of Portugal (1415–1578)}}

[[File:Portuguese Carracks off a Rocky Coast RMG BHC0705 (1).png|thumb|alt=Painting showing 10 Portuguese vessels near a fortified walled town with a tower rising above the harbor where several vessels lie at anchor, wooded hills stretch into the background, and a distant island appears offshore, with colors shifting from brown-green in the foreground to blue in the distance.|The ''Portuguese Carracks Off a Rocky Coast'' painting displays Portuguese [[Carrack|vessels]] of the [[Age of Discovery]] during the 16th century.]]

After King [[Ferdinand I of Portugal|Ferdinand I]]’s death in 1383, his minor daughter [[Beatrice of Portugal|Beatrice]] became his legal successor under the regency of [[Leonor Teles]].{{sfnp|Pinto|2025|pp=158-165}} Leonor's unpopularity, triggered a [[1383–1385 Portuguese interregnum|crisis]] compounded by fears that the marriage between Beatrice and [[John I of Castile]] {{ill|Treaty of Salvaterra de Magos|pt|Tratado de Salvaterra de Magos|lt=could lead}} to the loss of Portugal's independence.{{sfnp|Pinto|2025|p=160}} In 1385, [[John I of Portugal|John of Aviz]], a [[royal bastard]] and brother of Ferdinand, was {{ill|Cortes of Coimbra (1385)|pt|Cortes de Coimbra de 1385|lt=elected}} king and later defeated the Castilians in the [[Battle of Aljubarrota]] consolidating the [[House of Aviz]]'s rule over the country.{{sfnp|da Cruz Coelho|2025|pp=337-341}}{{sfnp|Monteiro|2020a|pp=63-65}}{{sfnp|Monteiro|2020b|pp=70-74}} In 1386, during his reign, Portugal cemented its [[Anglo-Portuguese Alliance|alliance with England]] with the signing of the [[Treaty of Windsor (1386)|Treaty of Windsor]], making it the [[List of military alliances|oldest standing alliance in the world]].{{sfnp|Childs|2024|pp=24-26,36-37}}<ref>{{cite web |last1=Fergusson |first1=George |last2=Trowbridge |first2=Benjamin |title=History's unparalleled alliance: the Anglo-Portuguese Treaty of Windsor, 9th May 1386 |website=History of Government |publisher=gov.uk |date=9 May 2016 |url=https://history.blog.gov.uk/2016/05/09/historys-unparalleled-alliance-the-anglo-portuguese-treaty-of-windsor-9th-may-1386/ |url-status=live |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20240607175621/https://history.blog.gov.uk/web/20240607175621/https://history.blog.gov.uk/2016/05/09/historys-unparalleled-alliance-the-anglo-portuguese-treaty-of-windsor-9th-may-1386/ |archive-date=Jun 7, 2024 |access-date=20 April 2026}}</ref> The dynasty contributed to the conditions for Portugal's maritime expansion,{{sfnp|Barros Cardoso|da Silva Lima|2024|pp=388–410}} elevating it in European politics and culture.{{sfnp|Lima|2018|pp=81–97}}{{sfnp|Montes|2019|pp=305-307}}{{sfnp|Soares|2019|pp=313-337}} Portugal acquired its first colonies by [[Portuguese conquest of Ceuta|conquering Ceuta]] in [[North Africa]] in 1415.{{sfnp|Ferrarini|Mocelim|2024|pp=518–540}}{{sfnp|dos Santos|2017|p=25}}{{sfnp|Monteiro|2017|pp=63-78}} Portugal was among the first to start the Age of Discovery,{{sfnp|Mukherjee|2025|pp=357-361|loc="...despite its peripheral position in European affairs, Portugal and not Spain kick-started Europe's 'discovery' project."}}{{sfnp|Casimiro|2020|pp=1–17|loc="In the Early Modern age, Portugal was among the first European countries to engage in overseas trade and colonial ventures."}}{{efn|The concept of discovery that applies to Portuguese overseas activities in the 15th and 16th centuries emerged in the [[Treaty of Alcáçovas]]. After the 1480s it became the main criterion for territorial claims. Before then, Portuguese expansion centered on conquest, [[religious war]], and [[Catholic Church and the Age of Discovery|forced conversion]]. Prior to the treaty, documents made no reference to discovery.{{sfnp|Menez|2025|pp=51,59,60-61,73}}}} and its [[Portuguese nautical science|scientific advances]] influenced other European countries' overseas expansions.{{sfnp|Fontes da Costa|Leitão|2009|pp=35–56}}{{sfnp|Vila-Santa|2024|pp=332-334}}

Portugal was the first and the last colonial power in the era of modern European [[colonialism]],{{efn|Portugal's colonial empire endured from the [[Portuguese conquest of Ceuta|conquest of Ceuta]] in 1415 to the [[handover of Macau]] in 1999. Historian L. Veracini states that Portugal was the first and the last explicitly colonial power.{{sfnp|L'Estoile|2023|p=xii}}{{sfnp|Veracini|2023|p=12}}{{sfnp|Schriffl|2022|p=487}}}} and by the mid-16th century it controlled a network of possessions stretching from Lisbon to [[Nanban trade|Japan]] and [[Portuguese Timor|Timor]] through [[Colonial Brazil|Brazil]].{{sfnp|Veracini|2023|p=28}} Throughout the 15th and 16th centuries, [[European exploration of Africa#Portuguese|Portuguese explorers]] sailed the coast of Africa, establishing trading posts [[Economic history of Portugal#Expansion of the Portuguese empire (15th and 16th centuries)|for commodities]] especially [[gold]] and [[slavery in Portugal|slaves]], explored the [[Indian Ocean]] and eastern Asia, established trade routes in most of southern Asia taxing most trade criss-crossing the Indian Ocean.{{sfnp|Phillips|2014|p=22}}{{sfnp|Smith|Boscariol|2022|pp=609–638}}{{sfnp|Veracini|2023|pp=20-23}} However, by the 16th century its enterprise in Asia was already in decline.{{sfnp|Mueller|Ayello Leite|2020|pp=1,23}} The 1494 [[Treaty of Tordesillas]] divided newly encountered non-European territories between Portugal and Spain along a [[Meridian (geography)|meridian]] west of [[Geography of Cape Verde|Cape Verde]], while the 1529 [[Treaty of Zaragoza]] extended this partition to the Pacific Ocean.{{sfnp|de Grijs|2020|pp=465-494}} 

In 1498, [[Portuguese discovery of the sea route to India|Vasco da Gama reached India by sea]], and two years later [[Discovery of Brazil|Pedro Álvares Cabral landed in Brazil]] and claimed it for Portugal.{{sfnp|Mathew|2024|p=[https://books.google.com/books?id=UBOSEQAAQBAJ&pg=PA110 110]}}{{sfnp|Veracini|2023|p=22}} During the 15th century, Portugal established the transatlantic slave-trade circuits and, by the mid-19th century, had become one of the longest-active and a significant participant in the [[Atlantic slave trade]].{{sfnp|da Silva|2021|pp=77-104}}

=== Iberian Union and Restoration ===
{{Main|Iberian Union|History of Portugal (1640–1777)}}

[[File:Coroação de D. João IV.png|alt=Engraving of the coronation of John IV of Portugal|thumb|The coronation of King [[John IV of Portugal|John IV]] in Lisbon]]

The 1578 death of [[Sebastian, King of Portugal|King Sebastian]] in [[Battle of Alcácer Quibir|battle]] and the subsequent 1580 death of [[Henry, King of Portugal|King Henry]] without a named heir, precipitated a [[Portuguese succession crisis of 1580|succession crisis]].{{sfnp|Sousa|2024|pp=81-100}}{{sfnp|Arroyo|2016|pp=1-21}}{{sfnp|McTaggart|2021}} [[Philip II of Spain]], whose mother was [[Isabella of Portugal]], was one of the candidates for the throne.{{sfnp|Arroyo|2016|pp=1-21}}{{sfnp|Barrio Gozalo|2025|pp=303-305}} In 1581 he was {{ill|Cortes of Tomar|lt=acclaimed|pt|Cortes de Tomar de 1581}} king of Portugal, creating a [[Iberian Union|personal union]] between the two kingdoms.{{sfnp|Arroyo|2016|pp=1-21}}{{sfnp|Barrio Gozalo|2025|pp=303-305}}{{sfnp|Stella|1999|pp=411-438}} This deprived Portugal of an independent foreign policy and led to the [[Dutch-Portuguese War]].{{sfnp|Lousada|2024|pp=185-201}}{{sfnp|da Costa|2021|pp=29-52}}{{sfnp|Borschberg|2024|pp=48-72}}

In 1641, [[John IV of Portugal|John, Duke of Braganza]], was proclaimed king by the [[Portuguese Cortes|Cortes]] after an uprising led by [[Forty Conspirators|some nobles]] in the [[Restoration of the Independence (Portugal)|year before]], ending the Iberian Union under the [[House of Habsburg]], and beginning the rule of the [[House of Braganza]].{{sfnp|Hespanha|1993|pp=31-32}}{{sfnp|	Oliveira|2002|pp=714-717}}{{sfnp|Tengwall|2011|pp=448-459}} Following the [[Portuguese Restoration War]], Spain [[Treaty of Lisbon (1668)|recognised Afonso VI]] as king of Portugal.{{sfnp|Dores Costa|2005|pp=1-14}}{{sfnp|Mitchell|2024|p=265}}{{sfnp|Esteves Soares|2025|pp=153-155,174-175}} During the reign of [[John V of Portugal|John V]], the large influx of Brazilian gold into the royal treasury, chiefly through the [[royal fifth]], produced a [[resource curse]] that weakened industry and cereal production, created a [[gold rush]] from Portugal to Brazil, and led to prolonged economic stagnation, permanent reduction of income growth, and the interruption of the institutional and economic progress achieved in the 17th century.{{sfnp|Kedrosky|Palma|2025|pp=730–766}}

After the depletion of gold revenues, Portugal was left with a weak industrial base and backward institutional and educational structures, a condition exacerbated by [[Sebastião José de Carvalho e Melo, 1st Marquis of Pombal]], who emerged as the country's {{lang|la|[[de facto]]}} ruler in the aftermath of the [[1755 Lisbon earthquake|1755 earthquake and tsunami]], a [[List of megathrust earthquakes|natural disaster]] that shook the kingdom and recognized as one of the worst to ever occur. Pombal's failed industrialisation efforts, education policies that weakened human-capital formation, and [[Suppression of the Society of Jesus|expulsion of the Jesuits]], reducing student numbers and contributing to Portugal's decline in literacy. Pombal's instrumentalisation of the [[Portuguese Inquisition|Inquisition]] for political purposes, helping to entrench an extractive system that outlasted his rule. The nature of the existing political institutions contributed to Portugal's sustained divergence from Western Europe despite comparable [[family values]] and high [[state capacity]], with partial recovery beginning from around the mid-20th century alongside the continuation of colonialism.{{sfnp|Kedrosky|Palma|2025|pp=730–766}}{{sfnp|Stadheim|2023|p=777}}{{sfnp|Santos|Rijo|2022}}

=== Constitutional monarchy ===
{{Main|History of Portugal (1777–1834)|History of Portugal (1834–1910)}}

[[File:Sessão das Cortes de Lisboa.jpg|thumb|upright|The [[Constituent Cortes of 1820]] approved the [[Portuguese Constitution of 1822|first constitution of Portugal]].|alt= A large historical painting shows a crowded parliamentary chamber in the middle of a heated debate during the Constituent Cortes of 1820. Dozens of formally dressed men gesture, argue, and rise from their seats beneath towering crystal chandeliers and a richly decorated ceiling, while officials sit at a central desk below a grand canopy and throne.]]

In 1807, Portugal refused [[Napoleon]]'s demand to join the [[Continental System]] of embargo against the United Kingdom; [[Invasion of Portugal (1807)|an invasion]] led by [[Jean-Andoche Junot|General Junot]] followed, and Lisbon was captured in 1807.{{sfnp|Joor|2016|p=57}}{{sfnp|Neves|2016|pp=101-104}} During the [[French invasions of Portugal|Napoleonic invasions]], the [[Transfer of the Portuguese court to Brazil|Portuguese royal family transferred]] the court to [[Rio de Janeiro]], in Brazil, making it the capital between 1808 and 1821.{{sfnp|Cavalcanti|2007|pp=149–199}} British intervention in the [[Peninsular War]] helped support Portuguese independence, and all French troops were expelled by 1812.{{sfnp|Ertl|2008|p=303}}

In 1820, an [[Liberal Revolution of 1820|uprising]] in [[Porto]] sought to establish a constitution for Portugal and led to the return of King [[John VI of Portugal|John VI]] and his court to mainland Portugal in 1821.{{sfnp|Cardoso|2022}} Although the [[Portuguese Constitution of 1822|1822 Constitution]] was adopted, the process was marked by compromise and efforts at consensus, which allowed absolutist forces to regain strength, culminating in [[Miguelist]] counter-revolts against liberalism, prompting Prince [[Miguel I of Portugal|Miguel]]'s exile.{{sfnp|Cardoso|2022}}{{sfnp|Lousada|2022|pp=287-288}} After John VI's death in 1826, his eldest son, [[Pedro I of Brazil]], briefly reigned as Pedro IV of Portugal, granted the [[Constitutional Charter of 1826|1826 Charter]], and abdicated in favour of his minor daughter, [[Maria da Glória]], on the condition that she marry Miguel, who was appointed regent.{{sfnp|Lousada|2022|pp=288-290}} Although Miguel's return was under the condition of swearing allegiance to the Charter, he soon dismantled liberal institutions and was proclaimed king in 1828, leading to the [[Liberal Wars]], after which Pedro forced Miguel's abdication in favour of Maria and his exile causing absolutist ideology to wane.{{sfnp|Lousada|2022|pp=288-290}}

Under the {{ill|Constitutional monarchy (Portugal)|pt|Monarquia constitucional (Portugal)|lt=constitutional monarchy}}, the country faced economic crises, political instability, and several coups d'état.{{sfnp|Birmingham|2018|pp=117–145}} Portugal's African {{ill|Colonisation of Africa (Portugal)|pt|Colonização portuguesa de África|lt=colonial expansion}} culminated in the [[1890 British Ultimatum]], which thwarted its [[Pink Map|ambitions]].{{sfnp|Patrício|2013|pp=11371-11397}}{{sfnp|Teixeira|1987|pp=687-719}} The ultimatum and the [[Anglo-Portuguese Treaty of 1891|1891 Anglo-Portuguese Treaty]], compounded by existing turmoil, led to political erosion and dealt a blow to the monarchy.{{sfnp|Patrício|2013|pp=11397-11408}}{{sfnp|Teixeira|1987|pp=687-719}}

=== First Republic and {{lang|pt-PT|Estado Novo}} ===
{{Main|First Portuguese Republic|Estado Novo (Portugal){{!}}{{lang|pt-PT|Estado Novo|cat=no}} (Portugal)}}

[[File:Proclamação República Portuguesa.jpg|thumb|[[José Relvas]] proclaiming the republic on [[5 October 1910 revolution|5 October 1910]], at [[Lisbon City Hall]]|alt=Black-and-white photograph of José Relvas proclaiming the Republic together with several formally dressed men leaning over the balcony railing of Lisbon City Hall and looking down onto a square surrounded with buildings.]]

In 1908, [[Carlos I of Portugal|King Carlos I]] and [[Luís Filipe, Prince Royal of Portugal|Luís Filipe, Prince Royal]], [[Lisbon Regicide|were assassinated]] by [[Portuguese Republican Party|republican]] partisans.{{sfnp|Corrêa da Silva|2019|pp=63-86}}{{sfnp|Barral Martínez|2026|pp=123-130}} In 1910, the monarchy was [[5 October 1910 revolution|replaced]] with a republic.{{sfnp|Corrêa da Silva|2019|pp=63-86}}{{sfnp|Pires|2017|pp=331-354}}{{sfnp|Wheeler|1972|pp=177-193}} During [[World War I]], Portugal [[Portugal during World War I|fought for the Allies]].{{sfnp|de Meneses|2025|pp=241-246}} The war increased political instability and deepened the economic crisis creating further chaos and unrest during the [[First Portuguese Republic|First Republic]].{{sfnp|Ribeiro de Meneses|Aires Oliveira|2023|pp=273–292}}{{sfnp|Ferraz|2023a|pp=278–301}}{{sfnp|Madureira|2010|pp=647-663}} These conditions led to the [[28 May 1926 coup d'état|overthrow of the Republic]] in 1926 and the establishment of the {{lang|pt-PT|{{ill|Ditadura Militar (Portugal)|pt|Ditadura militar portuguesa|lt=Ditadura Militar}}}},{{sfnp|Ferraz|2023b|pp=3-5,9-10}}{{sfnp|Schiavon|2011|pp=229–238}}{{sfnp|Lousada|2026|pp=181-201}} later the {{lang|pt-PT|[[Ditadura Nacional]]}},{{sfnp|Gazalle Cavichioli|2025|pp=4-6}}{{sfnp|Moreira|2020|p=493}} which [[1933 Portuguese constitutional referendum|evolved]] into the authoritarian nationalist [[corporative]] dictatorship of the {{lang|pt-PT|[[Estado Novo (Portugal)|Estado Novo]]}},{{sfnp|Cruz|1985|pp=347-353}}{{sfnp|Borges Santos|2025|pp=838-852}} under [[António de Oliveira Salazar]].{{sfnp|Leal|2016|pp=128-148}}{{sfnp|Reis Santos|2024|pp=400-401,406-421}}{{sfnp|Ferreira da Cunha|2006|pp=187-207}}

During the {{lang|pt-PT|Estado Novo}}, Portugal [[Portugal during World War II|remained neutral]] in [[World War II]], after which the postwar end of colonial legitimacy and the growth of African independence movements increasingly challenged Portuguese rule overseas.{{sfnp|Golson|2020|pp=79-110}}{{sfnp|Schriffl|2022|pp=474-476}} The regime introduced reforms intended to strengthen ties between the [[metropole]] and the [[Overseas province|colonies]], yet the [[Portuguese Empire|empire]] was no unified customs and economic area.{{sfnp|Schriffl|2022|pp=474-477}} [[Indigenous peoples]] already [[second-class citizen]]s,{{sfnp|Moutinho|2000|pp=87-132}} saw limited improvements in living conditions further fuelled nationalist movements,{{sfnp|Moutinho|2000|pp=87-132}}{{sfnp|Garcia|2021|pp=45-60}}{{sfnp|Cann|2021|pp=91-105}} ultimately leading to the [[Portuguese Colonial War]] from {{ill|Attacks in the north of Angola in 1961|pt|Ataques ao norte de Angola em 1961|lt=1961}} to 1974.{{sfnp|Aires Oliveira|2017}}{{sfnp|Júnior|2021|pp=121-137}}{{sfnp|Rodrigues|2021|pp=107-128}}{{sfnp|Lousada|Oliveira|2025|pp=15-45}}

=== Return to democracy ===
{{Main|Carnation Revolution|Third Portuguese Republic}}

[[File:Ondanks een verbod wilden leden van de Portugese Maoïstische beweging MRPP op 31, Bestanddeelnr 927-7289.jpg|thumb|left|Armoured vehicles in Lisbon's [[Rossio Square]], in 1975|alt=Black-and-white nighttime photograph of several armoured military vehicles lined up in Rossio Square, with soldiers standing beside them near the Column of Pedro IV and bright neon signs glowing in the background.]]

In 1974, a [[Timeline of the Carnation Revolution|military coup]] overthrew the {{lang|pt-PT|Estado Novo}} regime,{{sfnp|Pimentel|2024|pp=121-146}}{{sfnp|Andrade|2024|pp=253-254}} and started the Carnation Revolution.{{sfnp|Varela|Alcântara|2015|p=151-152}}{{sfnp|Pimentel|2024|pp=121-146}}{{sfnp|Leite Pinto|2016|pp=309-343}} This initiated the [[Portuguese transition to democracy|transition to democracy]] and the dissolution of Portugal's colonial empire,{{sfnp|Costa Pinto|Paris|2023|pp=7-18}}{{sfnp|Mendes|Teixeira|2026}} from the independence of the [[Portuguese-speaking African countries|African colonies]] to the [[Handover of Macau]] in 1999.{{sfnp|Esgalhado|2025|pp=52-68}}{{sfnp|MacQueen|2018|pp=172-175,178}}{{sfnp|Mendes|Steenhagen|2024|pp=297-312}} This period was marked by social, military, and political turmoil that culminated in [[Hot Summer of 1975|street politics and violence]] and led Portugal to near civil war.{{sfnp|Palacios Cerezales|2017|pp=688-709}}{{sfnp|Palacios Cerezales|2003|pp=1127-1157}}{{sfnp|Ferreira|2025|pp=223-237,241-242}} The [[Coup of 25 November 1975|1975 coup]] cleared the way for the approval of a new constitution and the holding of elections, beginning to subside tensions.{{sfnp|Pinto|2008|pp=305-332}}{{sfnp|Costa Pinto|Paris|2023|pp=11,17-19}}{{sfnp|Rezola|2012|pp=656-658}}

After the revolution, in the late 1970s and throughout the 1980s, under the influence by new economic insights, Portugal set about dismantling [[socialist]] policies from that period and moving towards [[neoliberalism]].{{sfnp|Noronha|2022|pp=113–128}} In 1986, Portugal joined the [[European Economic Community]] (EEC), which led to a considerable growth of its economy.{{sfnp|Dooley|2018|pp=75-82}} The [[2010–2014 Portuguese financial crisis]] resulted in an international bailout and intense [[austerity]] policies, causing lasting social impacts.{{sfnp|Pedroso|2014}}{{sfnp|Fonseca|Lourenço|Francisco|Crespo|2023|pp=314–326}}

== Geography ==
<!-- If you want to expand this section, please add new info into the main articles: "Geography of Portugal" and "Protected areas of Portugal," your work there will be very appreciated. -->
{{Main|Geography of Portugal}}

[[File:Portugal topographic map-en.svg|alt=Refer to caption|upright|thumb|A [[topographic map]] of Portugal]]

Portugal's territory comprises mainland Portugal and the Azores and Madeira archipelagos.{{sfnp|Ramos|Ramos-Pereira|2020|p=4}} The mainland, commonly referred to as Continental Portugal, is located in the southwest of the Iberian Peninsula in [[Southwestern Europe]], while Madeira and the Azores lie in the [[Atlantic Ocean]].{{sfnp|Ramos|Ramos-Pereira|2020|p=4}}<ref>{{cite web |url=https://portaldiplomatico.mne.gov.pt/en/about-portugal |publisher=Ministry of Foreign Affairs |author=<!-- not stated --> |website=Diplomatic Portal |access-date=27 May 2026 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20251027183927/https://portaldiplomatico.mne.gov.pt/en/about-portugal |title=About Portugal |archive-date=27 October 2025 |url-status=live}}</ref> Portugal's land area is {{convert|92,225|km2|sqmi|0|abbr=on}}, although [[Portuguese law]] defines the country's size as {{convert|156,597|km2|sqmi|0|abbr=on}} which includes about {{convert|64,000|km2|sqmi|0|abbr=on}} of ocean waters.{{sfnp|Bessa Pacheco|2014|pp=105-121}}{{sfnp|Ramos|Ramos-Pereira|2020|p=4}}{{efn|The territory of Portugal includes its land, [[internal waters]], and [[territorial waters#Territorial sea|territorial sea]].{{sfnp|Ferrão|2016|pp=123-134}}}} [[Exclusive economic zone of Portugal|Portugal's exclusive economic zone]] extends {{Convert|1,727,408|km2|mi2|abbr=on}}, making it one of the largest in the European Union.{{sfnp|Baptista|Neto|Braverman|Steinberg|2025|pp=805-827}}{{sfnp|Calado|Frazão Santos|Quintela|Fonseca|2024}} The country is over two-thirds [[wilderness]], almost one-fourth agricultural, and the remainder [[human settlement]]s.{{sfnp|''Portugal: forest profile''|2021|p=1}} 

Portugal's highest point is the summit of [[Mount Pico]], located on [[Pico Island]] in the Azores, which rises to an elevation of {{convert|2351|m|ft|abbr=on}} above sea level.{{sfnp|Melo|Neto Torres|Ferreira Fernandes|Marques Lameirinhas|2020}}{{efn|The highest peak in [[Continental Portugal|mainland Portugal]] is [[Torre (Serra da Estrela)|Torre]] in [[Serra da Estrela]], at an elevation of {{convert|1993|m|ft}}.{{sfnp|Andrade|Mourato|Ramos|2021}}}} Portugal can be divided into four morphostructural units: the [[Hesperian Massif]], which occupies most of mainland Portugal; the sedimentary borderlands of the massif,  forming the [[Lusitanian Basin|Lusitanian]] and [[Algarve Basin|Algarve]] basins; the Lower Tagus and Alvalade sedimentary basins; and the volcanic submarine ranges that form the Azores and Madeira.{{sfnp|Ramos|Ramos-Pereira|2020|pp=4-5,13}} 

Its [[Geology of the Iberian Peninsula#Portugal|geological]] and [[Geomorphology|geomorphological]] features are largely the product of the [[Variscan]], responsible for the formation of the Hesperian Massif, and later the Tethys–Atlantic cycle, responsible for the remaining units.{{sfnp|Ramos|Ramos-Pereira|2020|pp=4-13,29}} Regional tectonic processes affected mainland Portugal's morphostructural units differently, producing relief variations that subdivide the territory into ten regional geomorphological units shaped by the different regional impacts of the [[Cenozoic]] tectonics, regional asymmetries of climate in the [[Paleogene]], [[Neogene]], and [[Quaternary]], and the varied response of different lithologies to tectonic activity and climatic variability.{{sfnp|Ramos|Ramos-Pereira|2020|pp=13-29}}

Continental Portugal is predominantly low-altitude, with over 70% of the territory lying below {{convert|400|m|ft|abbr=on}} and less than 12% rising above {{convert|700|m|ft|abbr=on}} of elevation. Its geography is structured by the [[Tagus]] River, which enters from Spain and flows into the [[Tagus Estuary Natural Reserve|Tagus Estuary]], as 95% of areas exceeding {{convert|400|m|ft|abbr=on}} are situated to the north of the river while the regions south of the Tagus, encompassing [[Alentejo]] and the [[Algarve]], have 62% of the lands below {{convert|200|m|ft|abbr=on}}. The territory north of the Tagus is marked by mountains and plateaus incised by river valleys, whereas the south is distinguished by rolling plains.{{sfnp|Ramos|Ramos-Pereira|2020|pp=13–25}}

The Madeira archipelago comprises the islands of [[Madeira Island|Madeira]] and [[Porto Santo]], together with the [[Desertas Islands|Desertas]] and [[Savage Islands]], all of which are of [[Geology of Madeira|volcanic origin]]. Approximately one-third of Madeira Island lies above {{convert|1000|m|ft}} in elevation, and its landscape is characterised by a dense network of deep valleys with slopes rising several hundred metres, as well as streams that originate in the island's centre and diverge towards the coast, sustained by abundant rainfall. The steep gradient and very high rainfall is a cause of [[flash floods]] with high [[sediment transport]].{{sfnp|Ramos|Ramos-Pereira|2020|pp=25–26}}

The Azores is an archipelago composed of nine volcanic islands which, from west to east, are [[Flores Island (Azores)|Flores]], [[Corvo Island|Corvo]], [[Faial Island|Faial]], [[Pico Island|Pico]], [[São Jorge Island|São Jorge]], [[Graciosa]], [[Terceira Island|Terceira]], [[São Miguel Island|São Miguel]], and [[Santa Maria Island|Santa Maria]]. The islands retain much of their volcanic landforms, most visibly in volcanic cones and in the lakes that shape the scenery of the islands especially São Miguel, Flores, Terceira, Pico, and Corvo. Although rainfall is abundant, most streams in the Azores are temporary. The small size of the [[Drainage basin|catchment]]s, the [[Stream gradient|slopes' steepness]], and [[deforestation]] accentuates the [[Discharge regime|torrential regime]] of the rivers during periods of intense precipitation causing [[Soil erosion|erosion problems]] and [[Natural disasters in the Azores|floods]].{{sfnp|Ramos|Ramos-Pereira|2020|pp=26–28}}{{sfnp|''Portugal: forest profile''|2021|p=1}}{{sfnp|Pereira|Mendes|Dias|2022}}

=== Climate ===
{{Main|Climate of Portugal|Climate change in Portugal}}

{{Image frame|align=right|border=yes|caption=The [[Köppen climate classification|Köppen climate types]] of Portugal's terrain{{sfnp|Beck|McVicar|Vergopolan|Berg|2023|p=3}}|content=

{{stack
|{{CSS image crop

|{{CSS image crop
|alt = Refer to caption and to adjacent text

}}

The climate of mainland Portugal and Madeira, together with their surrounding waters, is [[Mediterranean climate|Mediterranean]], whereas the Azores and its adjacent waters mainly have a [[Oceanic climate|temperate oceanic climate]].{{sfnp|Rohli|Vega|Henderson|2024|p=271}}{{sfnp|Adinolfi|Loprieno|Demory|Zeman|2025}}{{sfnp|Mora|Vieira|2020|pp=33–46}} Portugal's terrain has a diverse variety of regional climates for its size.{{sfnp|Mora|Vieira|2020|pp=33–46}} The average temperature in Portugal varies from {{convert|4-20|°C|°F|1}} in the mainland, around {{convert|18|°C|°F|1}} in the Azores, and {{convert|9-19|°C|°F|1}} in Madeira.{{sfnp|Mora|Vieira|2020|p=36}}{{sfnp|Rusu|2023|pp=5-15}}{{sfnp|Pinheiro de Carvalho|Ragonezi|Oliveira|Reis|2022}}

The Iberian Peninsula is located at the southern margin of the temperate zone and at the northern margin of the subtropical [[High-pressure area|high-pressure zone]]. Additionally, Portugal's climate is influenced by the seasonal latitudinal shift of the [[jet stream]], which directly impacts the trajectory of the [[polar front]]. Typically in the winter, the jet stream moves southwards and Portugal comes under the influence of the polar front, producing colder temperatures. When the polar front moves northward, Portugal comes under the influence of the [[Azores High]] bringing [[atmospheric stability]] during the summer.{{sfnp|Mora|Vieira|2020|pp=33–46}}

{{ill|Wildfires in Portugal|pt|Incêndios florestais em Portugal|lt=Wildfires}} remain a serious challenge in Portugal, with nearly 30% of those recorded in 2024 having an undetermined origin and arson accounting for almost five-sixths of the total burned area.{{sfnp|Sedano|San-Miguel-Ayanz|Broglia|Durrant|2025|p=102}} Between 2014 and 2024, Portugal recorded an annual average of 12,496 fires and {{convert|112455|ha|sqmi|0|abbr=on}} burned, making it the European Union country with both the highest average number of wildfires and the greatest average burned area over that period.{{sfnp|Sedano|San-Miguel-Ayanz|Broglia|Durrant|2025|p=13}} [[Climate change in Portugal|Climate change]] is projected to raise average temperatures in Portugal by as much as {{convert|3-4|°C|°F|1}} by 2100 relative to the 1990s–2010s average,{{sfnp|Carvalho|2024}}{{sfnp|Carvalho|Meirelles|Henriques|Navarro|2022|p=353}}{{sfnp|Adinolfi|Loprieno|Demory|Zeman|2025}}  with impacts in [[Water supply and sanitation in Portugal|water]],{{sfnp|Claro|Fonseca|Fraga|Santos|2024}} [[Biodiversity of Portugal|ecosystem]],{{sfnp|Pedersen|Dias|Kok|van Vuuren|2024}} [[Effects of climate change on agriculture|agriculture]],{{sfnp|Lima|Bento|Lemos|Nogueira|2023}} [[Effects of climate change on human health|health]],{{sfnp|Teixeira|Carvalho|Rocha|Pereira|2026}} and [[Climate security|security]].{{sfnp|Araújo|Ramos|Soares|Melo|2022|pp=2279–2293}}{{sfnp|Cramer|Guiot|Fader|Garrabou|2018|pp=972–980}}

=== Biodiversity ===
{{Main|Biodiversity of Portugal}}

[[File:Miradouro da Cascata do Arado.jpg|right|thumb|The [[Peneda-Gerês National Park]] in [[North Region, Portugal|Northern Portugal]] is the only national park in Portugal.{{sfnp|Cruz|Sánchez|Suárez|2024}}|alt=A wide view of a forested mountain valley in the Peneda-Gerês National Park under grey clouds, with rocky slopes on both sides, a stream running through the centre, and a small stone bridge partly hidden among the trees]]

Portugal is located in the [[Mediterranean basin]], a [[biodiversity hotspot]].{{sfnp|Solomou|2025|p=147}} It is home to six terrestrial ecoregions: [[Azores temperate mixed forests]], [[Cantabrian mixed forests]], [[Madeira evergreen forests]], [[Iberian sclerophyllous and semi-deciduous forests]], [[Northwest Iberian montane forests]], and [[Southwest Iberian Mediterranean sclerophyllous and mixed forests]].{{sfnp|Dinerstein|Olson|Joshi|Vynne|2017|pp=534–545}} Almost a quarter of its land area is included in the [[Natura 2000]] network.{{sfnp|Frazão-Moreira|Martins|2023|pp=93-111}} Native species such as [[Quercus ilex|holm oak]], [[cork oak]], [[Pinus pinea|stone pine]], and [[maritime pine]] make up 72% of the total forested area of continental Portugal.{{sfnp|Ferreira|Ribeiro|Pereira|2026}}

[[Upwelling]] occurs at the coast creating the [[Nortada]], a summer wind along the coast.{{sfnp|Ramos|Pires|Sousa|Trigo|2013|pp=38-51}} 73% of the freshwater fish species in Portugal and Spain are endemic, a higher percentage than elsewhere in Europe.{{sfnp|Rodeles|Galicia |Miranda|2016}} Among Portugal's [[protected areas of Portugal|protected areas]] are the [[Southwest Alentejo and Vicentine Coast Natural Park]], one of Europe's last remaining stretches of wild coastline, and [[Montesinho Natural Park]], which preserves one of the few intact Mediterranean mountain landscapes.{{sfnp|Bastos|Dias|Baptista|Batista|2012|pp=99–116}}{{sfnp|Castro|2009|pp=151–154}}

Geographical and climatic conditions facilitate the spread of invasive plant species. Over one-fourth of extant plant species in continental Portugal are [[Introduced species|exotic]].{{sfnp|Oliveira-Costa|2022|pp=604,620-621}} Portugal has one of the highest numbers of threatened animal and plant species in Europe.<ref>{{cite web |publisher=IUCN |author=<!-- not stated --> |title=IUCN Red List of threatened species |version=2025-2 |url=https://www.iucnredlist.org/statistics |website=IUCN Red List |access-date=22 April 2026 |year=2025}}</ref> Portugal is an important stopover for migratory birds, as its wetlands serve as key wintering and stopover sites for many waders due to their location on the [[East Atlantic Flyway]].{{sfnp|Belo|Dias|Jara|Almeida|2023|pp=141–149}} Some mammalian species and subspecies of Portugal such as [[red deer]], [[Iberian ibex]], [[wild boar]], [[red fox]], [[Iberian wolf]], and [[Iberian lynx]] were once widespread throughout the country, but intense hunting, habitat degradation, and growing pressure from agriculture and livestock largely reduced their populations in the 19th and 20th centuries.{{sfnp|Bencatel|Sabino-Marques|Álvares|Moura|2019|pp=70,72-73,96,104,106,112,226-227}} There are three mammalian species and subspecies, such as the [[Portuguese ibex]] that became extinct, but some mammalian species, such as the [[Iberian hare]], have been re-expanding their native range.{{sfnp|Santos|Ceríaco|2025|p=3}}{{sfnp|Bencatel|Sabino-Marques|Álvares|Moura|2019|pp=156-157,226-227}}

== Government and politics ==
{{Main|Government of Portugal|Politics of Portugal}}

{{multiple image
| alt1 = António José Seguro smiling beside a large Portuguese coat of arms.
| alt2 = Luís Montenegro smiling besides a Portuguese flag.
| caption_align     = center
| image1            = António José Seguro 2026-04-21 (cropped).png
| width1            = 154
| caption1          = [[António José Seguro]]<br />[[President of Portugal|President]]
| image2            = Luís Montenegro September 2025.jpg
| width2            = 151
| caption2          = [[Luís Montenegro]]<br />[[Prime Minister of Portugal|Prime Minister]]
| total_width       = 305
}}

Portugal has been a [[Semi-presidential system|semi-presidential]] representative democratic [[republic]] since the ratification of the [[Constitution of Portugal|Constitution of 1976]].{{sfnp|''Constitution of the Portuguese Republic''|2005|loc= Part IV: Guaranteeing and revision of the Constitution, Final and transitional provisions, Article 296, Clause 2}}{{sfnp|Neto|2023|pp=122-125,132}}{{sfnp|Tavares de Almeida|2010|pp=1535-1539}} The Constitution divides the core [[Political structure|structures]] of the Portuguese political system among four [[Sovereignty body (Portugal)|sovereignty organs]]: the [[President of Portugal|president]], the [[Government of Portugal|government]], the [[Assembly of the Republic (Portugal)|Assembly of the Republic]], and the [[Judiciary of Portugal|courts]].{{sfnp|Fontes|2013|pp=93-94}} There is [[universal suffrage]] for adults over 18 years of age, with a [[secret ballot]] for all elected offices.{{sfnp|Tavares de Almeida|2010|pp=1535-1537}} Portugal operates a competitive multi-party system at the national, regional, and local levels.{{sfnp|de Sousa|da Cruz|Fernandes|2023|pp=1-29}}{{sfnp|Ruel|2021|pp=85-91}}{{sfnp|Calca|Ruel|2024|pp=704-718}} There have been trends towards [[Democratic backsliding|autocratisation]].{{sfnp|Marina|Altman|Fernandes|God|2026|p=41|loc="The eight countries that are very close to becoming autocratizers are: Bulgaria, Cyprus, Namibia, Portugal, Russia, Sierra Leone, Sudan, and Vanuatu. Three of the eight countries are new on the Watchlist this year: Bulgaria, Portugal, and Vanuatu."}}<ref>{{Cite web |title=Portugal |author=The Global State of Democracy |url=https://www.idea.int/democracytracker/country/portugal |access-date=29 April 2026 |publisher=International IDEA |date=June 2025 |quote=Between 2019 and 2024, it experienced declines in multiple factors of Representation, Rights and Rule of Law. |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20260331023341/https://www.idea.int/democracytracker/country/portugal |archive-date=31 March 2026 |url-status=live}}</ref>

The [[List of heads of state of Portugal|head of state]] of Portugal is the president who is elected to a five-year term by direct, [[universal adult suffrage]].{{sfnp|Narciso|2023|p=210}}{{sfnp|Mendonça|2023|p=52}}{{sfnp|Tavares de Almeida|2010|pp=1536-1537}} The president is usually regarded as politically independent and carries [[Moderating power (Portugal)|moderating powers]] aimed at ensuring the regular functioning of institutions and constitutional guarantees;{{sfnp|Graça Feijó|2021|pp=164,166}}{{sfnp|Gomes|Albergaria|Canotilho|2021|pp=478-479,517}}{{sfnp|Feijó|2019|pp=39-41}}{{efn|The president also holds exceptional powers besides the moderating powers, which are not exclusive to the office.{{sfnp|Gomes|Albergaria|Canotilho|2021|p=514}}}} in addition, it includes [[commander-in-chief|supreme command]] of the armed forces,{{sfnp|Gomes Canotilho|Moreira|1991|pp=101-106}} and shapes political opinions and agendas.{{sfnp|Grimaldi|2023|pp=196-205,222-228,380-396}}{{sfnp|Graça Feijó|2021|pp=90-95}}{{sfnp|Amorim Neto|Anselmo|2024|pp=704-721}} The current president of Portugal is [[António José Seguro]]; he {{ill|Portugal presidential inauguration|pt|Tomada de posse do Presidente da República Portuguesa|lt=took office}} after winning the runoff vote of the [[2026 Portuguese presidential election]].<ref>{{cite news|title=António José Seguro toma posse como Presidente da República|trans-title=António José Seguro is sworn in as president|access-date=12 August 2026|date=9 March 2026|location=Lisbon|publisher=[[Lusa News Agency]]|language=pt-PT|url=https://www.lusa.pt/article/46713307/|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20260812153458/https://www.lusa.pt/article/46713307/|archive-date=12 August 2026|url-status=live}}</ref><ref>{{cite news |first1=Andrei |last1=Khalip |first2=Sergio |last2=Goncalves |location=Lisbon |url=https://www.reuters.com/world/portugal-votes-presidential-runoff-with-socialist-poised-victory-2026-02-08/ |title=Portugal elects Socialist as president by landslide, but far right grows |publisher=Reuters |date=8 February 2026 |access-date=29 April 2026}}</ref>

{{multiple image
| direction         = vertical
| width             = 220
| image1            = Lisbon, Belém Palace.JPG
| caption1          = The [[Belém Palace]], residence and workplace of the president
| alt1              = Photograph of Belém Palace. A large pale pink palace with tiled roofs sits behind a walled terrace and the Afonso de Albuquerque Square, with statues, trimmed hedges, and a few cars passing along the street in front.
| image2            = Palácio de São Bento (Assemblée nationale) (9307363528).jpg
| caption2          = The [[São Bento Palace]], seat of the [[Assembly of the Republic (Portugal)|Assembly of the Republic]]
| alt2              = Photograph of São Bento Palace. A large white neoclassical government building with tall columns and sculpted pediments rises above a green lawn under a clear blue sky, with the Portuguese flag flying from the roof.
}}

Portugal's legislative body is the [[Assembly of the Republic (Portugal)|Assembly of the Republic]], a [[unicameralism|unicameral]] parliament.{{sfnp|Goes|Leston-Bandeira|2022|p=[https://books.google.pt/books?id=_DeKEAAAQBAJ&pg=PA138 138]}}{{sfnp|Losada Maestre|2021|p=227}} It consists of a single chamber with a minimum of 180 seats and a maximum of 230, elected by popular vote every four years or when dissolved.{{sfnp|Narciso|2023|p=209}}{{sfnp|Goes|Leston-Bandeira|2022|pp=[https://books.google.pt/books?id=_DeKEAAAQBAJ&pg=PA138 138-140]}}{{sfnp|Neto|2023|pp=131-132}} The members of parliament represent the whole country and not the constituencies for which they are elected.{{sfnp|Silva|Giovannetti|2022|p=69}} The Assembly of the Republic is dominated by three political parties, the [[Social Democratic Party (Portugal)|Social Democratic Party]] (PSD), [[Chega]] (CH), and the [[Socialist Party (Portugal)|Socialist Party]] (PS).{{sfnp|Mendes|2025|pp=469-494}}

As the head of government, the [[Prime Minister of Portugal|prime minister]] directs government policy, leads the [[Council of Ministers (Portugal)|Council of Ministers]] which includes ministers and junior ministers that execute government policy, and is appointed by the president in light of electoral results after consulting with the parties with parliament seats.{{sfnp|Belchior|Silveira|2023|pp=310–329}}{{sfnp|Silveira|Silva|2023|p=150}}{{sfnp|Neto|2023|p=123}} Portugal's current prime minister is [[Luís Montenegro]], who took office after the [[Democratic Alliance (Portugal, 2024)|AD – PSD/CDS Coalition]] won enough seats to form a minority government following the [[2024 Portuguese legislative election]].<ref>{{cite news |first1=Catarina |last1=Demony |first2=Sergio |last2=Goncalves |location=Lisbon |url=https://www.reuters.com/world/europe/portugals-minority-government-be-sworn-stability-doubtful-2024-04-02/ |title=Portugal's minority government sworn in, stability doubtful |publisher=Reuters |date=2 April 2024 |access-date=29 April 2026}}</ref>

Portugal has a [[Civil law (legal system)|civil law system]] based on [[Roman law]] and on [[Canon Law]] and influenced by [[Bürgerliches Gesetzbuch|German civil law]].{{sfnp|Hamza|2012|pp=2–4}} The Constitution is the [[supreme law]] of Portugal.{{sfnp|Violante|2023|pp=133-136}} In the Portuguese legal system, [[private law|private civil law]] and [[criminal law]] are codified in the {{lang|pt-PT|[[Portuguese Civil Code|Código Civil]]}} and the {{lang|pt-PT|[[Penal Code of Portugal|Código Penal]]}} respectively.{{sfnp|Mączyński|Moura Vicente|2017|p=2433}}{{sfnp|Líbano Monteiro|2024|pp=111–118}} Portugal's court system is organised into judicial, administrative, and fiscal branches with three levels of jurisdiction and separate courts of last appeal, while the [[Constitutional Court (Portugal)|Constitutional Court]] is responsible for constitutional review and oversight of elections, referendums and political parties.{{sfnp|Narciso|2023|p=211}}

=== Administrative divisions ===
{{Main|Administrative divisions of Portugal}}

Portugal is a [[Centralized government|highly centralised]] [[unitary state]] comprising two autonomous regions, as well as seven [[NUTS statistical regions of Portugal|regions]] on the [[Continental Portugal|mainland]]. Subnational government in Portugal is currently organised through municipalities ([[concelhos]]), civil parishes ([[freguesias]]), and intermunicipal communities ([[Intermunicipal communities of Portugal|comunidades intermunicipais]]).
