=== Foreign relations ===
{{Main|Foreign relations of Portugal}}

[[File:Diplomatic foreign relations of Portugal.svg|thumb|alt=Map of international relations of Portugal|[[Diplomatic missions of Portugal]]<ref>{{cite web |url=https://portaldiplomatico.mne.gov.pt/en/bilateral-relations/countries |website=Diplomatic Portal |author=<!-- not stated --> |title=Countries |publisher=Ministry of Foreign Affairs |author-link=Ministry of Foreign Affairs (Portugal) |access-date=6 May 2026 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20260414062402/https://portaldiplomatico.mne.gov.pt/en/bilateral-relations/countries |archive-date=14 April 2026 |url-status=live}}</ref>{{resizediv|95%|{{legend|#377eb8|Countries that host a Portuguese Embassy or Consulate}}
{{legend|#c0c0c0|Countries that do not host Portuguese diplomatic missions}}
{{legend|#4daf4a|Portugal}}}}|upright=1.2]]

A member state of the [[United Nations]] since 1955,{{sfnp|Castaño|2015|pp=9–38}} Portugal is a [[International organization membership of Portugal|member of almost all major international organisations]],{{sfnp|Pereira|2018|p=281}} and is among the countries that founded [[NATO]] in 1949,{{sfnp|Teixeira|1999|pp=15–41}} the [[OECD]] in 1961,<ref>{{cite web |url=https://www.oecd.org/en/about/legal/text-of-the-convention-on-the-organisation-for-economic-co-operation-and-development.html |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20240701192433/https://www.oecd.org/en/about/legal/text-of-the-convention-on-the-organisation-for-economic-co-operation-and-development.html |archive-date=1 July 2024 |url-status=live |title=Convention on the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development |publisher=Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development |author=<!-- not stated --> |access-date=29 April 2026}}</ref> [[European Free Trade Association|EFTA]] in 1960,{{sfnp|Andresen-Leitão|2004|pp=285–312}} and the [[Community of Portuguese Language Countries]] (CPLP) in 1996, an international organisation of countries with Portuguese as an official language.{{sfnp|Freixo|2006|pp=35–54}}

Portugal has a stable foreign policy that is the result of its history, geography, and specific foreign policy choices. It is structured around six principal regional and policy priorities: Europe, NATO and relations with the [[Portugal–United Kingdom relations|United Kingdom]] and with the [[Portugal–United States relations|United States]], the [[Portuguese-speaking world]], the importance of and support for Portuguese communities abroad, the internationalization of the [[Economy of Portugal|national economy]], and the strengthening of [[multilateralism]]. Portugal regards the success of [[European integration]] as paramount and is prepared, where a consensus exists among European Union member states, to endorse policies that go against with the country's national interests;{{sfnp|Pereira|2018|pp=269–283}} it also supports general disarmament, the dismantling of political-military blocs, and the creation of a collective security system aimed at establishing an international order grounded in peace and justice among peoples.{{sfnp|Coutinho|Piçarra|2019|pp=629-632}}

Portugal has two territorial disputes, both of which are with Spain: the Spanish town of [[Olivenza]] which has been claimed by Portugal since the [[War of the Oranges|19th century]], and the surrounding waters of the Portuguese Savage Islands which have been claimed by Spain since the 15th century. Despite causing moments of tension between the two countries, the [[Portugal–Spain relations|relationship between the two countries]] remains excellent.{{sfnp|Gómez Adeva|2017|pp=752–765}}{{sfnp|Santos Sánchez|2022|pp=23–44}}<ref>{{Cite web |title=Spain |url=https://portaldiplomatico.mne.gov.pt/en/bilateral-relations/general-countries/spain |access-date=29 April 2026 |website=Diplomatic Portal |publisher=Ministry of Foreign Affairs |author-link=Ministry of Foreign Affairs (Portugal) |author=<!-- not stated --> |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20260209132834/https://portaldiplomatico.mne.gov.pt/en/bilateral-relations/general-countries/spain |archive-date=9 February 2026 |url-status=live}}</ref>

=== Military ===
{{Main|Portuguese Armed Forces}}

[[File:NRP Bartolomeu Dias F333 in Hernesaari as seen from Verkkokauppa in Helsinki, Finland, 2023 April.jpg|thumb|[[Portuguese Navy]] frigate NRP Bartolomeu Dias|alt=Photograph of NRP Bartolomeu Dias (F333) of the Portuguese Navy docked by the quays of Hernesaari, Helsinki, Finland, with the Portuguese flag flying at the stern and calm blue water stretching out to the open sea beyond.]]

The [[Portuguese Armed Forces]] consist of three branches commanded by the {{lang|pt-PT|[[Armed Forces General Staff (Portugal)|Estado-Maior-General das Forças Armadas]]}} (Armed Forces General Staff): the {{lang|pt-PT|[[Portuguese Navy|Marinha]]}} (Navy), {{lang|pt-PT|[[Portuguese Army|Exército]]}} (Army), and {{lang|pt-PT|[[Portuguese Air Force|Força Aérea]]}} (Air Force).{{sfnp|Carreiras|2025|pp=185-186}} In addition to the three branches of the armed forces, there is the {{lang|pt-PT|[[National Republican Guard (Portugal)|Guarda Nacional Republicana]]}} (National Republican Guard), a [[gendarmerie]], comprising {{Nts|23042}} personnel in 2025, under the authority of both the [[Ministry of National Defence (Portugal)|Defence]] and the [[Ministry of Home Affairs (Portugal)|Home Affairs]] ministries.{{sfnp|Lloyd-Jones|Cerezales|2007|p=90}}{{sfnp|Departamento de Informação da Organização do Estado e do Emprego Público|2025a}} The Portuguese military serves as a self-defence force, takes part in humanitarian and peace missions undertaken by the international organisations to which Portugal belongs, and cooperates in civil defence missions.{{sfnp|Carreiras|2025|pp=183-197}} In recent years, the Portuguese military have carried out several [[NATO]] and [[European Union]] missions worldwide.<ref>{{Cite web |title=Onde estamos missões no estrangeiro |url=https://www.exercito.pt/pt/onde-estamos/no-mundo |publisher=Exército Português |access-date=29 April 2026 |author=<!-- not stated --> |language=pt-PT |trans-title=Where we are: missions abroad}}</ref> The [[List of countries with highest military expenditures|Portuguese military budget]] in 2025 was estimated at more than $6 billion, representing 2% of GDP.{{sfnp|''Defence expenditure of NATO countries (2014–2025)''|2025}}

As of 2025, the three branches numbered {{Nts|23589}} military personnel.{{sfnp|Departamento de Informação da Organização do Estado e do Emprego Público|2025b|p=7}} Until 1999, military service was compulsory for men at age 18 becoming officially suspended during peace time and replaced by one mandatory day of sensibilization about the Armed Forces called {{lang|pt-PT|{{ill|National Defence Day (Portugal)|pt|Dia da Defesa Nacional|lt=Dia da Defesa Nacional}}}} (National Defence Day).{{sfnp|Resende|2009|pp=11–24}} Since 1992 women may serve in all branches of the armed forces. The Portuguese military became fully professional in 2004.{{sfnp|Committee on Women in the NATO Forces|2008|pp=1–3}}

=== Law enforcement ===
{{Main|Law of Portugal|Judiciary of Portugal|Law enforcement in Portugal|Crime in Portugal}}

[[File:Polícia Judiciária (Portugal) headquarters, Lisbon 04.jpg|thumb|The headquarters of the {{lang|pt-PT|[[Polícia Judiciária]]}} (Judiciary Police), in Lisbon|alt=Photograph of the headquarters of the Judicial Police (PJ). A large modern white-and-grey office building with irregular window patterns stands on a corner under an overcast sky, with parked motorcycles, a few pedestrians, and a crosswalk in the foreground.]]

In Portugal, public prosecution is conducted by the [[Public Prosecution Service (Portugal)|Public Prosecution Service]] which is headed by the {{ill|Prosecutor General of Portugal|pt|Procurador-Geral da República (Portugal)|lt=prosecutor general}}.{{sfnp|Narciso|2023|pp=208–212}} The main police organisations of Portugal are the {{lang|pt-PT|Guarda Nacional Republicana}} (National Republican Guard), the {{lang|pt-PT|[[Polícia de Segurança Pública]]}} (Public Security Police), a civilian police force that works in urban areas; and the {{lang|pt-PT|[[Polícia Judiciária]]}} (Judiciary Police), a senior criminal police body, under the authority of the [[Ministry of Justice (Portugal)|Minister of Justice]] and endowed with administrative autonomy.{{sfnp|Lloyd-Jones|Cerezales|2007|p=90}}{{sfnp|Rocha Gonçalves|Durão|2017|p=4}}{{sfnp|Cardoso|2020|pp=189–193}}

Portugal has {{ill|List of prisons in Portugal|pt|Lista de prisões portuguesas|lt=49 correctional facilities}} run by the {{ill|Directorate-General for Reintegration and Prison Services|pt|Direção-Geral de Reinserção e Serviços Prisionais|lt=Directorate-General for Reintegration and Prison Services}} (DGRSP).{{sfnp|Conde|Sousa|Pita|Souto|2026|pp=1–19}} The facilities are classified by security level, and by degree of management complexity, and often house inmates with different legal statuses, risk levels, and criminal trajectories within the same facilities.{{sfnp|Conde|Sousa|Pita|Souto|2026|pp=1–19}} Despite following a reintegration-oriented penal model, Portugal retains high prison occupancy and an average sentence length of 31.3 months and one of the world's highest [[Prisoner suicide|prison suicide]] rates representing around 30% of the 60 to 70 [[Death in custody|deaths]] occurring annually.{{sfnp|Conde|Sousa|Pita|Souto|2026|pp=1–19}}{{sfnp|Lança Pereira|2026|pp=181-197}}

=== Human rights ===
{{Main|Human rights in Portugal}}

Portugal has a tradition of a humanistic criminal justice.{{sfnp|Miranda Rodrigues|João Antunes|Fidalgo|Horta Pinto|2021|p=1}} The Portuguese Constitution defines the country as being one that is based on human dignity.{{sfnp|Martins|Roque|2013|pp=312-314}} It abolished [[Capital punishment in Portugal|capital punishment]] and [[life imprisonment]] in the 19th century,{{sfnp|Miranda Rodrigues|João Antunes|Fidalgo|Horta Pinto|2021|p=1}} and forbids [[extradition]] in the case of either sentence possibly being imposed.{{sfnp|Costa|Caeiro|2021|pp=281-283,299-304}} The [[Portuguese Penal Code]] provides for a wide range of non-custodial sentences, with the aim of keeping a prison sentence a punishment of last resort.{{sfnp|Miranda Rodrigues|João Antunes|Fidalgo|Horta Pinto|2021|p=1}} Portuguese penitentiary laws have traditionally been progressive, and based on rehabilitation as the main goal of the implementation of such a sentence.{{sfnp|Miranda Rodrigues|João Antunes|Fidalgo|Horta Pinto|2021|p=1}}

Since the 1980s, Portugal has strengthened [[LGBTQ rights in Portugal|LGBTQ rights]] and become one of [[LGBTQ rights in Europe|Europe's most equal countries]], although [[LGBTQ history in Portugal|historical isolation and authoritarianism]] continues to shape social acceptance of {{ill|LGBTQ people in Portugal|pt|Homossexualidade em Portugal|lt=LGBTQ people}}.{{sfnp|Ramalho|2026|pp=203-232}}{{sfnp|Gato|Portugal|Sotero|Seabra|2025|pp=146-149,161}}{{sfnp|Neves|Borges|Ferreira|Correia|2025|pp=349-364}} In 2001, Portugal [[Drug policy of Portugal|decriminalised]] the personal possession and consumption of all drugs.{{sfnp|Rêgo|Oliveira|Lameira|Cruz|2021}} Portugal faces issues such as [[Police brutality|unwarranted use of force by law enforcement]],{{sfnp|''Visit report - Portugal - November 2024''|2025|pp=8–14}}{{sfnp|Casquilho-Martins|Belchior-Rocha|Alves|2022|pp=1-19}} [[Racism in Portugal|racism]] and discrimination against minorities,{{sfnp|''Survey on living conditions, origins and trajectories of the resident population 2023''|2023}}{{sfnp|Loza|2024|pp=29-37}} {{ill|Slavery in contemporary Portugal|pt|Escravatura moderna em Portugal|lt=migrant slavery}},<ref>{{cite web |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20260226071501/https://www.state.gov/reports/2025-trafficking-in-persons-report/portugal/ |archive-date=26 February 2026 |url-status=live |url=https://www.state.gov/reports/2025-trafficking-in-persons-report/portugal/ |title=2025 trafficking in persons report: Portugal |publisher=United States Department of State |publication-date=30 September 2025 |access-date=29 April 2026 |author=<!-- not stated -->}}</ref> restrictions on [[freedom of association]] and collective bargaining,{{sfnp|United States Department of State - Bureau of Democracy, Human Rights, and Labor|2025|p=3}} violations of wage, hour, and overtime laws,{{sfnp|United States Department of State - Bureau of Democracy, Human Rights, and Labor|2025|p=5}} and persistent structural and institutional 
deficiencies in the prison system.{{efn|Such as constrained resources, inadequate conditions, insufficient transparency, and the risk of non-compliance with [[Prisoners' rights in international law|international human rights standards]].{{sfnp|Lança Pereira|2026|pp=181-197}}}}

== Economy ==
<!-- If you want to expand this section, please instead add new info into the main article: "Economy of Portugal," your work there will be appreciated. -->
{{Main|Economy of Portugal}}

[[File:Lisboa June 2014-14a.jpg|thumb|[[Parque das Nações]], in Lisbon; an economic centre in Portugal|alt=Photography of Parque das Nações, in Lisbon. Modern waterfront buildings, including two tall glass-and-concrete towers with sail-shaped white roofs, can be seen across the Tagus River under a blue sky with few thin white clouds. Trees, a riverside promenade, pedestrians, and nearby commercial buildings line the waterfront.]]

Portugal is a [[high-income country]] with an [[advanced economy]] that follows the [[European social model#Mediterranean model|Mediterranean model]].<ref>{{cite web |url=https://datahelpdesk.worldbank.org/knowledgebase/articles/906519-world-bank-country-and-lending-groups |title=World Bank country and lending groups |publisher=World Bank Group |author=<!-- not stated --> |website=World Bank Open Data |publication-place=Washington, D.C. |access-date=29 April 2026}}</ref>{{sfnp|''World economic outlook: global economy in the shadow of war''|2026|p=99}}{{sfnp|Gentilucci|2020|p=181}} The country's economic policy is framed by strategic guidelines called {{ill|Major Options|pt|Grandes Opções do Plano|lt=Major Options}}, which are subsequently implemented through the [[government budget|State Budget]].{{sfnp|Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development|2023|p=64}} Portugal has the EU's [[List of sovereign states in Europe by GDP (nominal)|14th-largest economy by nominal GDP]] and the [[List of sovereign states in Europe by GDP (PPP)|12th-largest economy by PPP-adjusted GDP]].{{sfnp|''World economic outlook: global economy in the shadow of war''|2026|pp=39,120,123,127-128,137-138}} Its PPP-adjusted GDP per capita stood at to 81% of the EU average in 2025.<ref>{{cite web |doi=10.2908/PRC_PPP_IND_1 |doi-access=free |title=Purchasing power parities, price level indices, nominal and real expenditures by analytical categories - based on COICOP 2018 |url=https://ec.europa.eu/eurostat/databrowser/bookmark/ecfbff41-69cb-47d9-bb89-bdb52711fcd4?lang=en |publication-date=25 March 2026 |publisher=Eurostat |author=<!-- not stated --> |access-date=30 April 2026}}</ref> As of 2024, Portugal's service sector contributed the most for its economic output followed the industrial sector and its [[primary sector]].<ref name="sectorsGDP">{{cite web |title=Statistics Portugal - data base - indicator 0006612 |publisher=[[Instituto Nacional de Estatística (Portugal)|Statistics Portugal]] |author=<!-- not stated --> |publication-date=11 December 2025 |access-date=30 April 2026 |url=https://www.ine.pt/xportal/xmain?xpid=INE&xpgid=ine_indicadores&indOcorrCod=0006612&contexto=bd&selTab=tab2&xlang=en}}</ref> In 2025, Portugal [[List of countries by unemployment rate|unemployment rate]] was 5.8%.{{sfnp|''Employment statistics – 4th quarter of 2025''|2026}} As of 2024, its poverty rate after social transfers was 15.4% of the population while in 2023, the at-risk-of-poverty rate before social transfers stood at 40.3%.{{sfnp|''Income and living conditions – 2025''|2025}}{{sfnp|Tavares|Carmo|2025|p=13}} The [[Portuguese national debt|national debt of Portugal]] was estimated at 89.7% relative to GDP as of 2025.{{sfnp|''Excessive deficit procedure: 1st notification 2026''|2026}}

Portugal is part of the [[European single market]] which represents more than 450 million consumers.<ref>{{cite web |url=https://ec.europa.eu/growth/single-market_en |title=The European single market |date=5 July 2016 |publisher=European Commission |author=<!-- not stated --> |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20200409110216/https://ec.europa.eu/growth/single-market_en |archive-date=9 April 2020 |url-status=live |access-date=16 April 2026}}</ref> Portugal replaced the [[Portuguese escudo|escudo]] with the [[euro]] in 2002.<ref>{{cite web |url=https://www.ecb.europa.eu/euro/changeover/2002/html/index.en.html |author=<!-- not stated --> |access-date=16 April 2026 |title=Initial changeover (2002) |date=31 January 2023 |publisher=European Central Bank |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20260420131848/https://www.ecb.europa.eu/euro/changeover/2002/html/index.en.html |archive-date=20 April 2026 |url-status=live}}</ref> Its monetary policy is set by the [[European Central Bank]].<ref>{{cite web |url=https://www.bportugal.pt/en/page/what-monetary-policy-and-what-it-used |publisher=Banco de Portugal |author=<!-- not stated --> |access-date=16 April 2026 |title=What is monetary policy and what is it used for? |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20250831070832/https://www.bportugal.pt/en/page/what-monetary-policy-and-what-it-used |archive-date=31 August 2025 |url-status=live}}</ref> The country has been a part of the [[Eurozone]] since its inception.<ref>{{cite web |url=https://economy-finance.ec.europa.eu/euro/what-euro-area_en |title=What is the euro area? |publisher=European Commission |access-date=16 April 2026 |website=Economy and Finance |author=<!-- not stated --> |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20260331235803/https://economy-finance.ec.europa.eu/euro/what-euro-area_en |archive-date=31 March 2026 |url-status=live}}</ref> Portugal's [[central bank]] is the [[Banco de Portugal]] and it is part of the [[European System of Central Banks]].<ref>{{cite web |url=https://www.bportugal.pt/en/page/eurosystemescb |title=Eurosystem/ESCB |publisher=Banco de Portugal |author=<!-- not stated --> |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20250824125004/https://www.bportugal.pt/en/page/eurosystemescb |archive-date=24 August 2025 |url-status=live |access-date=16 April 2026}}</ref> In 2024, Portugal had a combined share of exports and imports that amounted to 90% of its total GDP.<ref>{{cite web |url=https://data.worldbank.org/indicator/NE.TRD.GNFS.ZS |title=Trade (% of GDP) |publisher=World Bank Group |author=<!-- not stated --> |publication-place=Washington, D.C. |access-date=30 April 2026 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20260425212634/https://data.worldbank.org/indicator/NE.TRD.GNFS.ZS |archive-date=25 April 2026 |url-status=live}}</ref> 

In February 2026, Portugal's main export markets were Spain at 26% and Germany at 13.9%. Its main exports are machinery and mechanical appliances, vehicles and other transportation equipment, base metals, and plastics. Portugal's main import markets in 2026 were Spain at 32.9% and Germany at 11.9%. Portugal's main imports are machinery and mechanical appliances, chemical products, agricultural products, and mineral fuels.{{sfnp|''International trade – March 2026''|2026}}

Since the 1990s, Portugal's economic model has been based on [[consumption (economics)|public consumption]] and [[economic development]] focused on exports, private [[investment (macroeconomics)|investment]] and the development of its [[high-tech]] sector. Consequently, business services have overtaken more traditional industries such as [[wine]] and [[Cork (material)|cork]] in export earnings.{{sfnp|''Financial Times special report: investing in Portugal''|2014|p=4}} Operated by the [[Euronext Lisbon]], Portugal's stock market, the [[PSI-20|PSI]], includes 16 major companies based on the country, including [[Sonae]], [[Mota-Engil]], [[Corticeira Amorim]], [[The Navigator Company]], and [[EDP Group|EDP]].<ref name="psi">{{cite web |url=https://live.euronext.com/en/markets/lisbon/equities-by-index/psi |title=PSI stocks |publisher=Euronext |author=<!-- not stated --> |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20260130012145/https://live.euronext.com/en/markets/lisbon/equities-by-index/psi |archive-date=30 January 2026 |url-status=live |access-date=16 April 2026}}</ref>

In 2025, Portugal ranked 16th among European Union member states in innovation by the [[European Innovation Scoreboard]], while placing 31st worldwide in the [[Global Innovation Index]].{{sfnp|European Commission: Directorate-General for Research and Innovation|2025|p=71}}{{sfnp|''Global Innovation Index 2025: innovation at a crossroads''|2025|p=68}} In 2024, neuroscience accounted for 12.5% of Portugal's scientific output, the second-highest national share, and the country ranked 23rd worldwide in total neuroscience output.<ref>{{cite journal |title=High-performers and specialists in neuroscience research |journal=Nature |date=3 October 2024 |volume=634 |issue=8032 |pages=S7 |doi=10.1038/d41586-024-03045-6 |pmid=39358538 |bibcode=2024Natur.634S...7.}}</ref> Among the research institutions based in the country are the {{ill|Gulbenkian Institute of Molecular Medicine|pt|Instituto Gulbenkian de Ciência}}, the [[International Iberian Nanotechnology Laboratory]], and the [[Champalimaud Foundation]] which was the world's 11th top non-profit institution in neuroscience research between 2019 and 2023.<ref>{{Cite web |title=Leading 25 NPO/NGO institutions |author=<!-- not stated --> |publisher=Nature Portfolio |date=2 October 2024 |access-date=30 April 2026 |url=https://www.nature.com/nature-index/supplements/nature-index-2024-neuroscience/tables/npo-ngo}}</ref>

=== Agriculture and fishery ===
{{main|Agriculture in Portugal|Fishing industry in Portugal}}

[[File:Ameixial - cork store (13532168205).jpg|alt=A long pile of harvested cork bark stacked in a grassy field.|thumb|The harvesting of [[Cork (material)|cork]] bark. The majority of the world's cork is produced in Portugal.{{sfnp|Rodrigues|Gevrek|Mota|2023|pp=19–20}}]]

The Portuguese agriculture sector accounted for 2.9% of the country's total GDP as of 2024.<ref name="sectorsGDP" /> Portugal's [[agricultural land]] encompassed an area of approximately {{convert|3697000|ha|km2|0|abbr=on}} as of 2023, of which 17.4% was dedicated to [[organic farming]], representing the fifth-highest share worldwide.{{sfnp|''World food and agriculture – statistical yearbook 2025''|2025|pp=45,348,368}} Despite the extent of the country's agricultural land, the diversity of Portugal's [[Edaphology|edaphoclimatic]] and [[Agroecology|agroecological]] conditions enables the cultivation of various agricultural products such as wheat, maize, and rice, with each one having an important role in the primary sector, and resulting in an agricultural self-sufficiency rate of 85% as of 2018.{{sfnp|Viana|Freire|Abrantes|Rocha|2021}}{{sfnp|Gabinete de Planeamento, Políticas e Administração Geral|2021|pp=75–76}}

Portugal ranks sixth in the world in [[List of countries by seafood consumption|seafood consumption per capita]], with each Portuguese person consuming on average {{convert|54|kg|lb|0|abbr=on}} of fish in 2023.{{sfnp|''Fishery and aquaculture statistics – yearbook 2023''|2025|p=92}} The high fish consumption in Portugal is due to tradition and cultural roots, politics, dynamics of the fish market system, and geography.{{sfnp|Murthy|Galli|Madeira|Moreno Pires|2023}} Strong demand for seafood makes the fisheries a valuable national asset.{{sfnp|Murthy|Galli|Madeira|Moreno Pires|2023}} While Portugal captured {{convert|185000|t|LT ST}} of fish in 2019, down from the {{convert|222000|t|LT ST|abbr=unit}} in 2010, fish and seafood represent the single highest import of biocapacity from abroad, amounting to {{val|1600000|fmt=commas}} [[global hectare]]s in total.{{sfnp|Murthy|Galli|Madeira|Moreno Pires|2023}}

=== Industry and services ===
{{main|Tourism in Portugal|:pt:Indústria automóvel em Portugal|l2 = Automotive industry in Portugal}}

[[File:Vehículos en el puerto de Setúbal, Portugal, 2019-05-24, DD 01.jpg|alt=Rows of newly manufactured cars parked at a port in front of a Grimaldi Lines vehicle carrier.|thumb|[[Volkswagen Autoeuropa]] cars in the Port of Setúbal]]

Portugal's manufacturing industry accounted for 21.2% of the country's total GDP as of 2024, down from an annual average of 26% over the period between 1953 and 1973.<ref name="sectorsGDP" />{{sfnp|Ferraz|2024|p=4}} The lower contribution of manufacturing to Portugal's economy has led to lower real GDP growth rates between 1974 and 2019 than during the period between 1950 and 1973.{{sfnp|Ferraz|2024|p=4}} As of 2025, Portugal's automobile industry produced the biggest share of the country's exports.{{sfnp|''International trade – March 2026''|2026}} The automobile industry accounts for 20.2% of the country manufacturing exports and 82.8% of the total value of its exports employing 43.247 workers in 2020.{{sfnp|''Automotive industry in Portugal''|2022|p=4}}

Portugal's service sector accounted for 76.5% of the country's total economic output as of 2024.<ref name="sectorsGDP" /> Tourism, retail, and telecommunication are all major industries.<ref name="psi" /> Tourism represented 11.8% of the Portugal's total GDP as of 2025.{{sfnp|''Tourism satellite account – 2025''|2026}} Portugal attracted nearly 30 million international tourists in 2025,{{sfnp|''Tourism statistics - 2025''|2026|p=2}} [[World Tourism rankings|ranking 15th]] in the world in that year for inbound tourism.<ref>{{Cite web |title=Global and regional tourism performance |url=https://www.untourism.int/tourism-data/un-tourism-tourism-dashboard |access-date=1 May 2026 |publisher=UN Tourism |author=<!-- not stated --> |publication-date=22 April 2026 |archive-date=2 December 2025 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20251202030537/https://www.untourism.int/tourism-data/un-tourism-tourism-dashboard |url-status=live}}</ref>

=== Infrastructure ===
{{Main|Transport in Portugal|Energy in Portugal}}

[[File:Alqueva dam.JPG|alt=Alqueva dam with a road across its crest holds back a reservoir in a rural landscape.|thumb|The [[Alqueva Dam]] is one of the largest strategic water reserves in Europe.{{sfnp|Santos|Pais Dias|2021|p=4}}]]

Portugal's road network includes a system of [[Roads in Portugal#Motorways|48 motorways]], which, as of 2023, ranked as the [[Evolution of motorway construction in European nations|seventh largest among 42 European countries]].{{sfnp|''Transport and communications statistics - 2024''|2025|p=12}}<ref>{{cite web|url=https://www.dgterritorio.gov.pt/recart/TRANSPORTES%20(Transporte%20rodovi%C3%A1rio)/CatalogoDasViasRodoviariasParte1.html|title=Catálogo das vias rodoviárias - parte 1|trans-title=Catalog of road routes - part 1|language=pt-PT|publication-date=4 April 2024|publisher=Direção-Geral do Território|publication-place=Lisbon|access-date=1 July 2026|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20260606205531/https://www.dgterritorio.gov.pt/recart/TRANSPORTES%20(Transporte%20rodovi%C3%A1rio)/CatalogoDasViasRodoviariasParte1.html|archive-date=6 June 2026|url-status=live|author=<!-- not stated -->}}</ref><ref>{{cite web |url=https://ec.europa.eu/eurostat/databrowser/view/ttr00002/default/table |doi=10.2908/TTR00002 |doi-access=free |publisher=Eurostat |author=<!-- not stated --> |access-date=17 April 2026 |title=Total length of motorways |date=16 April 2026}}</ref> The busiest [[List of airports in Portugal|Portuguese airports]] are [[Lisbon Airport]], [[Porto Airport]], [[Faro Airport]], [[Madeira Airport]], and [[Ponta Delgada Airport]].{{Sfnp|''Civil aviation yearbook 2023''|2023|p=58}} As of 2024, passenger and freight [[Rail transport in Portugal|rail transport]] in Portugal operated on {{convert|2526|km|mi|0|abbr=on}} of active [[List of railway lines in Portugal|railway lines]], with passenger services operated mainly by [[Comboios de Portugal]] (CP).{{sfnp|''Transport and communications statistics - 2024''|2025|p=11}}{{sfnp|Marques|Moro|Ramos|2025|pp=683–713}} The [[Port of Sines]] serves as Portugal's foremost [[List of ports in Portugal|container port]] and leads the country in cargo [[tonnage]].{{sfnp|Kazmi|Rocha|Brochado|2025|p=5}}

As of 2023, two-thirds of Portugal's energy supply was imported, representing the 11th-highest level of energy dependency in the European Union.{{sfnp|Observatório da Energia|Direção-Geral de Energia e Geologia: Direção de Serviços de Planeamento Energético e Estatística|Agência para a Energia: Direção de Formação, Informação e Educação|2025|pp=44–46}} The country is pursuing [[Renewable energy in Portugal|energy transition]] and has invested in the development of renewable energy sources such as the [[Aguçadoura Wave Farm]].{{sfnp|Khatiwada|Vasudevan|Santos|2022|p=12}}{{sfnp|Silva|Rusu|Guedes Soares|2018}} Portugal has expanded the role of renewable sources in its energy sector, with renewables accounting for 35.2% of energy consumption, up from 21.9% in 2007, and 78.1% of [[Electricity generation|total installed capacity]], compared with 59.3% in 2014.{{sfnp|Observatório da Energia|Direção-Geral de Energia e Geologia: Direção de Serviços de Planeamento Energético e Estatística|Agência para a Energia: Direção de Formação, Informação e Educação|2025|pp=44,53-54,76}} In 2021, the country completed the phase-out of coal-fired generation.{{sfnp|Moreira|2025}}{{sfnp|Martins|Moura|de Almeida|2022|p=18}}

== Demographics ==
{{Main|Demographics of Portugal}}

{{See also|Portuguese people}}

[[File:Puente de Don Luis I, Oporto, Portugal, 2019-06-02, DD 29-31 HDR.jpg|alt=View during the blue hour of the Luiz I Bridge and the city of Porto.|thumb|upright 1.2|[[Porto]] is the [[List of cities in Portugal|second largest city]] in Portugal, with [[Porto metropolitan area|its metropolitan area]] the country's second-largest.]]

In 2025, Portugal had a population of {{Nts|11424031}}, of whom {{Nts|9826492}} were Portuguese nationals. The remainder were foreign residents at {{Nts|1597539}}.{{sfnp|''Estimates of resident population in Portugal 2025''|2026}} Portugal is steadily aging and has one of the world's highest proportion of elderly citizens, comprising nearly one-fourth of its entire population,{{sfnp|United Nations Department of Economic and Social Affairs|2023|p=23}}<ref>{{Cite web |title=Population ages 65 and above (% of total population) |url=https://data.worldbank.org/indicator/SP.POP.65UP.TO.ZS |access-date=25 April 2026 |publisher=World Bank |author=<!-- not stated --> |website=World Bank Open Data |archive-date=23 March 2026 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20260323030148/https://data.worldbank.org/indicator/SP.POP.65UP.TO.ZS |url-status=live |publication-place=Washington, D.C.}}</ref> together with a female share of the population at 51.1%.{{sfnp|''Gender equality in Portugal: statistical bulletin 2025''|2025|pp=16–17}}<ref>{{Cite web |title=Population, female (% of total population) |url=https://data.worldbank.org/indicator/SP.POP.TOTL.FE.ZS |access-date=25 April 2026 |publisher=World Bank |author=<!-- not stated --> |publication-place=Washington, D.C. |website=World Bank Open Data |archive-date=1 March 2026 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20260301210058/https://data.worldbank.org/indicator/SP.POP.TOTL.FE.ZS |url-status=live}}</ref>

In 2025, Portugal had a fertility rate of 1.4, which is below the [[Sub-replacement fertility|replacement rate]] of 2.1 and is one of the [[List of countries by total fertility rate|world's lowest]].{{sfnp|''Estimates of resident population in Portugal 2025''|2026}}<ref>{{Cite web |title=Fertility rate, total (births per woman)|url=https://data.worldbank.org/indicator/SP.DYN.TFRT.IN?view=map |access-date=17 August 2026 |publisher=World Bank |author=<!-- not stated --> |publication-place=Washington, D.C. |website=World Bank Open Data }}</ref> Portugal has had a fertility rate below the replacement rate of 2.1 since the 1980s that has led to the country having a [[Population pyramid|median age]] of 45.8, one of the [[List of countries by median age|highest in the world]].{{sfnp|Surkyn|Lesthaeghe|2004|pp=45–86}}{{sfnp|''Estimates of resident population in Portugal 2025''|2026}}<ref>{{cite web |url=https://population.un.org/dataportal/data/indicators/67,49/locations/4,8,12,16,20,24,660,28,32,51,533,36,40,31,44,48,50,52,112,56,84,204,60,64,68,535,70,72,76,92,96,100,854,108,132,116,120,124,136,140,148,152,156,344,446,158,170,174,178,184,188,384,191,192,531,196,203,408,180,208,262,212,214,218,818,222,226,232,233,748,231,238,234,242,246,250,254,258,266,270,268,276,288,292,300,304,308,312,316,320,831,324,624,328,332,340,348,352,356,360,364,368,372,833,376,380,388,392,832,400,398,404,296,412,414,417,418,428,422,426,430,434,438,440,442,450,454,458,462,466,470,584,474,478,480,175,484,583,492,496,499,500,504,508,104,516,520,524,528,540,554,558,562,566,570,807,580,578,512,586,585,591,598,600,604,608,616,620,630,634,410,498,638,642,643,646,652,654,659,662,663,666,670,882,674,678,682,686,688,690,694,702,534,703,705,90,706,728,724,144,275,729,740,752,756,760,762,764,626,768,772,776,780,788,792,795,796,798,800,804,784,826,834,840,850,858,860,548,862,704,876,732,900,887,894,716/start/2024/end/2024/table/pivotbylocation?df=67e9fdb2-341a-4b63-8655-87fae18ae077 |author=United Nations Population Division |publisher=United Nations |website=Population Division Data Portal |title=World Population Prospects: The 2024 Revision |access-date=1 May 2026 |date=2024}}</ref> As of 2025, 23.3% of the population was aged 65 or older.{{sfnp|''Estimates of resident population in Portugal 2025''|2026}} Despite the effects of net migration, due to low fertility rates, Portugal's population is projected to drop to 8.3 million by 2100.{{sfnp|''Projections of resident population in Portugal 2025-2100''|2025}}

Historically a country of emigration,{{sfnp|Federal Research Division, Library of Congress|1993|pp=[http://countrystudies.us/portugal/48.htm 75-79]}} Portugal has been a net recipient of [[Immigration to Portugal|immigrants]] since 1993 except between 2011 and 2016 during the Portuguese financial crisis.{{sfnp|''30 years of 25 April: a statistical portrait''|2004|p=8}}{{sfnp|Pires|Vidigal|2020|pp=5,6}}{{sfnp|''Estimates of resident population in Portugal 2025''|2026}} Since 2016, Portugal has experienced a marked increase in immigration, with the proportion of non-nationals in the total population rising from 3.5% to 14.0% by 2025, living mainly in the Algarve and the [[Lisbon metropolitan area]], and with 24.5% of all births registered in 2024 being to foreign-born women.<ref>{{cite web |author=<!-- not stated --> |url=https://ec.europa.eu/eurostat/databrowser/bookmark/6b569dd8-b96c-4111-a727-8e49e1aad2c5?lang=en&createdAt=2026-04-25T18:59:34Z |title=Population on 1 January by age, sex and broad group of citizenship |doi=10.2908/MIGR_POP2CTZ |doi-access=free |publisher=[[Eurostat]] |access-date=25 April 2026 |date=9 April 2026}}</ref>{{sfnp|''Survey on living conditions, origins and trajectories of the resident population 2023''|2023}}<ref>{{Cite web |url=https://www.ine.pt/xportal/xmain?xpid=INE&xpgid=ine_indicadores&indOcorrCod=0012437&contexto=bd&selTab=tab2&xlang=en |title=Statistics Portugal - data base - indicator 0012437 |publisher=Statistics Portugal |author=<!-- not stated --> |access-date=25 April 2026 |date=29 April 2025}}</ref> In Portugal, White Portuguese constitute the largest [[Race (human categorization)|racial]] and [[Ethnicity|ethnic group]], representing 84.2% of the population, followed by multiracial Portuguese at 3.4%, [[Black Portuguese]] at 2.2%, [[Luso-Asians|Asian Portuguese]] at 0.7%, and the [[Romani people in Portugal|Romani]] at 0.6%.{{sfnp|''Survey on living conditions, origins and trajectories of the resident population 2023''|2023}}

In 2023, 88% of the Portuguese population lived in urban areas.<ref>{{Cite web |url=https://www.ine.pt/xportal/xmain?xpid=INE&xpgid=ine_indicadores&indOcorrCod=0008856&contexto=bd&selTab=tab2&xlang=en |title=Statistics Portugal - data base - indicator 0008856 |publisher=Statistics Portugal |author=<!-- not stated --> |access-date=25 April 2026 |date=9 August 2024}}</ref> The capital city, [[Lisbon]], had a population of {{Nts|658236}} as of 2025. It is part of Lisbon metropolitan area, the biggest [[Metropolitan areas in Portugal|metropolitan area]] of Portugal with 3.4 million people.<ref name="population2025">{{Cite web |url=https://www.ine.pt/xportal/xmain?xpid=INE&xpgid=ine_indicadores&indOcorrCod=0012917&contexto=bd&selTab=tab2 |title=Statistics Portugal - data base - indicator 0012917 |publisher=Statistics Portugal |author=<!-- not stated --> |access-date=22 June 2026 |date=22 June 2026}}</ref>

=== Religion ===
<!-- If you want to expand this section, please add new info into the main article: "Religion in Portugal," your work there will be very appreciated. -->
{{Main|Religion in Portugal}}

[[File:Batalha September 2021-2.jpg|thumb|alt=The main gothic facade of the Monastery of Batalha, Portugal|The [[Batalha Monastery]] has historically been used to transmit a religious link to Portugal's history.{{sfnp|Lima|2018|pp=81–97}}]]

Portugal has been a [[secular state]] {{ill|Law on the separation of church and state (Portugal)|pt|Lei da Separação do Estado das Igrejas|lt=since 1911}}, and it guarantees [[religious freedom]].{{sfnp|de Moura|2024}}{{sfnp|Botelho Moniz|2024|pp=85-99}}{{efn|The [[Constitution of Portugal|1976 Constitution]] guaranteed [[religious freedom]] without distinction between denominations and without any specific limits, breaking with earlier regimes of union, secularist neutrality, and preferential relations with the Catholic Church. However, the legal instruments of 1940 were affected in 1975, when it was introduced the dissolubility of Catholic marriage, and remained in force until 2004, when the government and the [[Holy See]] agreed a new Concordat.{{sfnp|Botelho Moniz|2024|pp=85-99}}}} Although Portugal has no [[state religion|official religion]], the [[Catholic Church in Portugal|Catholic Church]] has a history there that predates the country's formation and can be traced back to the 3rd century.{{sfnp|Carneiro|2009|pp=205–220}} According to the 2021 Census, 80.2% of the Portuguese population aged 15 and older were Catholic, while 14.1% are nonreligious.<ref name="Censos2021" />{{efn|Although Portugal is [[Catholic Church by country|overwhelmingly Catholic]], the majority are [[Cultural Christians|non-practicing]], a discrepancy that might have existed throughout its religious history.<ref name="Censos2021">{{cite web |url=https://www.ine.pt/xportal/xmain?xpid=INE&xpgid=ine_indicadores&indOcorrCod=0012311&contexto=bd&selTab=tab2&xlang=en |title=Statistics Portugal - data base - indicator 0012311 |publisher=Statistics Portugal |author=<!-- not stated --> |date=23 November 2022b |access-date=25 February 2026 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20250815222045/https://www.ine.pt/xportal/xmain?xpid=INE&xpgid=ine_indicadores&indOcorrCod=0012311&contexto=bd&selTab=tab2&xlang=en |archive-date=15 August 2025 |url-status=live}}</ref>{{sfnp|Coutinho|2023|pp=1-15}}{{sfnp|Dix|2025|p=20}}}}

The country has small [[Protestantism in Portugal|Protestant]], [[The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints in Portugal|Latter-day Saints]], [[Islam in Portugal|Muslim]], [[Hinduism in Portugal|Hindu]], [[Sikhism in Portugal|Sikh]], {{ill|Jehovah's Witnesses in Portugal|pt|Testemunhas de Jeová em Portugal
|lt=Jehovah's Witnesses}}, [[Baháʼí Faith in Portugal|Baháʼí]], [[Buddhism in Portugal|Buddhist]], and [[History of the Jews in Portugal|Jewish]] communities.<ref name="Censos2021" /> Influences from [[African Traditional Religion]] and Chinese Traditional Religion are also felt among many people, particularly in fields related to [[Traditional Chinese Medicine]].{{efn|Chinese medical science is well known in Portugal and is widely accepted by the people. Portugal is the first European Union country that specifically provided legislation for acupuncture and Chinese medical science.{{sfnp|Song|Gao|Jia|2021}}}} Even though Portugal has deep ties with Christianity, as of 2019<!--the year when the interviews were conducted--> the majority of its people were shown to be tolerant towards followers of other faiths, with the Muslim community perceiving itself as thoroughly integrated into Portugal and believing that the country provided conditions conducive to smooth integration.{{sfnp|de Carvalho|Neyroud|Pinto|Pinto|2025|pp=2-16}}

=== Languages ===
{{Main|Languages of Portugal}}
{{See also|Portuguese language}}

[[File:Genísio04.jpg|thumb|left|A sign in [[Mirandese language|Mirandese]] in [[Miranda do Douro]]|alt=Photograph of a white street sign mounted on a tan wall reads in Mirandese "Rue de L Cruzeiro" with "Rua do Cruzeiro" written in Portuguese below in script, and a small leaf logo at the top.]]

Portuguese is the official and predominantly spoken language in Portugal.{{sfnp|Branco|Grilo|Silva|2023|p=195}}{{sfnp|European Commission: Directorate-General for Education, Youth, Sport and Culture and Verian|2024|p=11}} It is one of 24 official and working [[languages of the European Union]].<ref>{{CELEX|01958R0001-20130701|text=Regulation No 1 determining the languages to be used by the European Economic Community}}</ref> Portuguese is the fifth-most widely spoken first language in the world, with around 250 million native speakers.{{sfnp|Branco|Grilo|Silva|2023|pp=195–198}}

[[Portuguese Sign Language]] is officially protected by the country's constitution.{{sfnp|Figueira-Cardoso|Grzegorczy|Marçalo|2025|pp=9-36}} The recognised regional languages and dialects include [[Mirandese language|Mirandese]], spoken in [[Terra de Miranda]], and [[Barranquenho]], in [[Barrancos]].{{sfnp|Figueira-Cardoso|Grzegorczy|Marçalo|2025|pp=9-36}} Portuguese people are typically bilingual, with 67.5% speaking at least two languages, and tend to be proficient non-native English speakers, with Portugal placed sixth globally for English proficiency in the 2025 [[EF English Proficiency Index]].{{sfnp|''Adult education survey – 2022''|2023}}{{sfnp|EF Education First|2025|p=9}}

=== Education ===
<!-- If you want to expand this section, please add new info into the main articles: "Education in Portugal" and "Higher education in Portugal," your work there will be very appreciated. -->
{{Main|Education in Portugal}}

[[File:Universidad de Coímbra, Portugal, 2012-05-10, DD 16.JPG|alt=Square of the University of Coimbra|thumb|The [[University of Coimbra]] in [[Coimbra]] is the first university in Portugal.]]

Portugal's education system is organised into [[preschool]] education; school education, encompassing {{ill|Basic education in Portugal|pt|Ensino básico em Portugal|lt=basic}}, {{ill|Secondary education in Portugal|pt|Ensino secundário em Portugal|lt=secondary}} and [[Higher education in Portugal|higher education]]; and {{ill|Extracurricular education in Portugal|pt|Educação extraescolar|lt=extracurricular education}}, which includes [[continuing education]].{{sfnp|Correia|2025|p=316}}{{sfnp|Barros|2025|pp=271–295}} Optional preschool is provided for all children between three and five years old,{{sfnp|European Commission: Directorate-General for Education, Youth, Sport and Culture|2025|p=10}}{{sfnp|Assembly of the Republic|2025}} after which school attendance is compulsory for 12 years.{{sfnp|Cabrito|2023}} Basic education lasts nine years and is divided into three cycles, while secondary education is organised into [[College-preparatory school|academic]] and [[Vocational education|vocational pathways]].{{sfnp|Cabrito|2023}} 

Higher education is provided by universities and [[Polytechnic (Portugal)|polytechnic institutions]] in accordance with the [[Bologna Process]], with the [[University of Lisbon]] being the country's largest university by enrolment.{{sfnp|Urbano|2011|pp=95-115}} A general requirement for higher education are the {{lang|pt-PT|[[Exames Nacionais do Ensino Secundário|exames finais nacionais do ensino secundário]]}}.{{sfnp|Coordinators and authors of the National Secondary Education Examinations|2024|p=11}} Most secondary-school students continue their studies at public higher education institutions or the [[Military Academy (Portugal)|Military Academy]], which also awards university degrees.{{sfnp|''State of education 2024''|2025|p=79}}{{sfnp|Fonseca|Encarnação|2012|pp=31-32}}{{sfnp|''Main results of RAIDES25 – first stage – enrolled students and international mobility – 2025/2026''|2026}}

Although other European countries began investing in education in the 19th century, Portugal's long-established national identity, stable borders and homogeneous population contributed to a lack of political commitment to educational development until the First Republic in the early 20th century;{{sfnp|Gomes|Machado|2021|pp=135-164}} many of its reforms were subsequently reversed under the {{lang|pt-PT|Estado Novo}}, whose deliberate neglect of education contributed to Portugal recording Europe's highest illiteracy rate in 1964;{{sfnp|Felgueiras|Amorim|2024}}{{sfnp|Alves|Lima|Pereira|2019|p=23}} following democratisation in 1974, education became more widely accessible and underwent modernisation.{{sfnp|Cabrito|2023}} 

Portugal lags behind most OECD countries on educational indicators, largely owing to the older working-age generation.{{sfnp|''State of education 2024''|2025|pp=117-123}} Fewer than two-thirds of Portugal's population have completed secondary or higher education.{{sfnp|''State of education 2024''|2025|pp=117-123}} Portugal's adult population aged 16 to 65 records the fifth-lowest level of knowledge and skills in the OECD, whereas school students perform at average levels.{{sfnp|Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development|2024a|p=2}}{{sfnp|Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development|2024b}}{{sfnp|Rosa|Maia|Mascarenhas|Teodoro|2020|pp=94–120}} In 2021, the national illiteracy rate among people aged 10 or over stood at 3.1%, ranging from 1.4% to 12.3% across the country's municipalities.{{sfnp|''Census 2021 - 16th population census. 6th housing census''|2022|pp=44-45}} Portugal spent 4.5% of its total GDP on education in 2022.<ref>{{cite web |title=Public expenditure on education by education level and programme orientation - as % of GDP |doi=10.2908/EDUC_UOE_FINE06 |doi-access=free |date=8 May 2026 |publisher=Eurostat |url=https://ec.europa.eu/eurostat/databrowser/bookmark/a36b249b-5030-4681-b7c9-93fc139acae2?lang=en |access-date=25 May 2026 |author=<!-- not stated -->}}</ref>

=== Health ===

{{Main|Health in Portugal|Healthcare in Portugal}}
[[File:Hospitais da Universidade de Coimbra em 2025 - Foto 02.jpg|alt=The Coimbra University Hospital, part of the Centro Hospitalar e Universitário de Coimbra, in Coimbra.|thumb|upright 0.8|The [[Centro Hospitalar e Universitário de Coimbra]] in [[Coimbra]] is the largest medical complex in Portugal.<ref>{{cite web |url=https://benchmarking-acss.min-saude.pt/MH_CapacidadeUtilizadaDashboard |publisher=[[Ministry of Health (Portugal)|Ministry of Health]] |author=<!-- not stated --> |publication-date=27 April 2026 |access-date=8 May 2026 |title=Monitorização mensal hospitais - capacidade utilizada |trans-title=Monthly hospital monitoring – utilized capacity |language=pt-PT}}</ref>]]
Portugal has a predominantly [[Universal health care|universal tax-funded health care]] system, called {{lang|pt-PT|{{ill|National Health Service (Portugal)|pt|Serviço Nacional de Saúde|lt=Serviço Nacional de Saúde}}}} (SNS), that operates alongside public-sector health insurance schemes called health subsystems funded mainly through employee and
employer contributions, as well as private voluntary health insurance.{{sfnp|Fronteira|Augusto|Maresso|2025|p=2}} Primary and hospital care are provided by both public and private entities, with the private sector and complementing services offered by the SNS.{{sfnp|Fronteira|Augusto|Maresso|2025|p=3}} In 2023, Portugal had the 3rd lowest public share on health spending in the European Union and over half of public hospitals in [[insolvency|technical insolvency]] in 2020.{{sfnp|Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development|European Observatory on Health Systems and Policies|2025|p=7}}{{sfnp|Dubas-Jakóbczyk|Ndayishimiye|Szetela|Sowada|2025|p=3}} Portugal has a high level of health inequity in the OECD, driven by limited public spending, uneven distribution of services, low household income, long waiting times, and policy shortcomings.{{sfnp|Fernandes|Furtado|Pereira|2024|pp=104–111}}

Portugal [[List of countries by life expectancy|ranked 23rd]] in the world in 2024 in life expectancy with 85.4 years for women and 79.8 years for men, and it had an [[infant mortality rate]] of 2.7 deaths per {{Nts|1000}} [[Live birth (human)|live births]].{{sfnp|Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development|European Observatory on Health Systems and Policies|2025|p=2}}{{sfnp|United Nations Inter-Agency Group for Child Mortality Estimation|2026|p=94}} In 2022, the leading cause of death were cardiovascular diseases, at 25.4%.{{sfnp|Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development|European Observatory on Health Systems and Policies|2025|p=2}} Portugal presents several adverse public-health indicators, including the 3rd lowest share of people reporting good health in the European Union, in 2024, at 54%, the highest alcohol consumption with {{convert|11.9|l|impgal usgal}} consumed per person, in 2020, and the highest share of adult population living a [[sedentary lifestyle]], in 2022, at 73%, a pattern reinforced by limited active-transport infrastructure and unsafe conditions for pedestrians and cyclists.{{sfnp|European Commission: Directorate-General for Education, Youth, Sport and Culture|2022|p=9}}{{sfnp|Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development|European Observatory on Health Systems and Policies|2025|pp=2-3,5-6}} The percentage of the Portuguese population suffering from moderate or severe [[food insecurity]] between 2022 and 2024 was on average 11.9%, which makes Portugal the third-highest country in [[Southern Europe]] for this indicator where the average stands at 5.9%.{{sfnp|''The state of food security and nutrition in the world 2025 – addressing high food price inflation for food security and nutrition''|2025|p=120}} Stakeholders have raised concerns over the effectiveness of food safety policies.{{sfnp|Rodrigues|Saraiva|García-Díez|Castro|2025|pp=5-7,12-13}}

== Culture ==
{{Main|Culture of Portugal|Mass media in Portugal}}

[[File:Desfile 12.jpg|alt=Women wearing red traditional Portuguese costumes with embroidered aprons and gold jewelry at the Romaria de Nossa Senhora da Agonia.|thumb|In [[Viana do Castelo]], women traditionally wear {{ill|Viana gold|pt|Ouro de Viana|lt=family heirlooms}} in festivities.]]

Portugal has developed a specific culture due to, initially before its existence, the influence from various civilisations that have crossed Europe, especially the Mediterranean.{{sfnp|Suisse|2026|pp=42–59}}{{sfnp|Arruda|2009|p=124}} Later, during the period of [[History of Portugal (1415–1578)|Portugal's engagement in the Age of Discovery]], cultural elements from outside of Europe were introduced and became a central element of Portuguese national identity.{{sfnp|Casimiro|2020|pp=1–17}}{{sfnp|Peralta|2026|pp=341-364}}

Portugal is known for [[Portuguese architecture|its heritage and architecture]],{{sfnp|Gholitabar|Alipour|Costa|2018|p=2}} [[Sanctuary of Fátima|sacred sites]],{{sfnp|Heidari|Yazdani|Saghafi|Jalilvand|2018|p=751}} [[List of festivals in Portugal|festivities]],{{sfnp|Carvalho|Costa|2022|pp=73-82}}{{sfnp|Charzyński|Łyszkiewicz|Musiał|2017|pp=95-96}} [[Portuguese poetry|poetry]],{{sfnp|Ladeira|2008|pp=43-72}} its music (especially the [[fado]]),{{sfnp|Carvalho|Madeira|Monteiro|Lopes|2023|p=2}} and [[Portuguese cuisine|cuisine]] (including [[Portuguese wine|its wine]]).{{sfnp|Costa e Silva|Elo|2017|pp=425–442}}{{sfnp|Charzyński|Łyszkiewicz|Musiał|2017|pp=85-100}} As of 2026, [[UNESCO]] inscribed [[List of World Heritage Sites in Portugal|17 properties in Portugal on the World Heritage List]].<ref>{{cite web |title=World Heritage - List |url=https://www.unesco.org/en/world-heritage/list |publisher=UNESCO |author=<!-- not stated --> |access-date=26 February 2026 |url-status=live |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20260206124451/https://www.unesco.org/en/world-heritage/list |archive-date=6 February 2026}}</ref> Portugal's [[national day]] is on 10 June, celebrated as the {{lang|pt-PT|Dia de Portugal, de Camões e das Comunidades Portuguesas}} ([[Portugal Day|Portugal, Camões, and Portuguese Communities Day]]).{{sfnp|João|2011|pp=19-34}}

=== Art and architecture===
{{main|Portuguese art|Architecture of Portugal}}

[[File:Lagos40 kopie.jpg|alt=Six paintings attributed to Nuno Gonçalves presenting a 58 people of the Court and various groups of Portuguese society at that time, gathered together around the double figuration of St. Vincent with expressive look of concentration on their faces, engaged in an act of veneration.|thumb|The ''[[Saint Vincent Panels]]'' ({{circa|1450's}}) by [[Nuno Gonçalves]] are one of the most significant works of Portuguese painting from the 15th century.{{sfnp|Vermeersch|Rousaki|Lycke|Valadas|2025}}]]

The history of visual art in Portugal dates back into the [[Art of the Upper Paleolithic|Paleolithic]]. An early evidence showing an attempt at depicting motion was found at the [[Prehistoric Rock Art Sites in the Côa Valley and Siega Verde]].{{sfnp|''Evaluation report of cultural properties - 1998''|1998|p=46}} Over time, foreign and native influences, together with advancements in manufacturing, developed distinctive art styles, such as the [[Manueline]] and [[Pombaline style|Pombaline]],{{sfnp|Câmara|1999|pp=197-214}} and also a number of crafts that are typical of Portugal, namely the {{lang|pt|[[azulejo]]}}, {{lang|pt|[[Gilded woodcarving in Portugal|talha dourada]]}}, and [[Portuguese pavement]].{{sfnp|Sandu|Murta|Neves|Pereira|2016|pp=47–71}}{{sfnp|Resende|2022|pp=206–227}}{{sfnp|Pereira|Conte|Esteves|Pais|2023|pp=3804–3815}}

Historically, religion, specifically Christianity, had an influential role in Portuguese art, as it was a recurrent theme widely employed in many art forms, such as in painting.{{sfnp|Vermeersch|Rousaki|Lycke|Valadas|2025}} Throughout the country's history, artwork in Portugal was typically done by local artists who, depending on location, followed different variations in style, giving Portugal a diverse array of artistic styles throughout the country, an example of this being the {{ill|Typical thatch houses of Santana|pt|Casas típicas de Santana|lt=thatch houses of Santana}}, in Madeira.{{sfnp|Silva|da Silva|2024}}

=== Literature ===
{{Main|Portuguese literature}}

[[File:Camões, por Fernão Gomes.jpg|thumb|alt=Drawing of Luís Vaz de Camões|upright|left|[[Luís Vaz de Camões]] was the writer of ''[[Os Lusíadas]]''.]]

Portugal has a literary tradition that predates the Portuguese language going back into the early 13th century.{{sfnp|Cohen|Parkinson|2009|pp=37–43}} Portuguese literature developed through song as well as the written page known as {{lang|pt-PT|[[cantigas]]}}.{{sfnp|Monteagudo|2007|pp=275-312}} The {{lang|pt-PT|cantigas}} drew practitioners from all social ranging from King [[Denis of Portugal|Denis I]] to [[Martin Codax]] who was a [[minstrel]].{{sfnp|Macedo|2009|p=2}} The earliest known work of literature produced by a Portuguese is the ''{{ill|Ora faz ost'o senhor de Navarra|ca|Ora faz ost’o senhor de Navarra}}'', a {{lang|pt-PT|[[Cantigas de escárnio e maldizer|cantiga de escárnio e maldizer]]}} written in [[Galician–Portuguese]] by [[João Soares de Paiva]] at around the year 1200.{{sfnp|Castro|2015|p=120}}{{sfnp|Miranda|2012|p=2}}

Portuguese literature developed under the influence of both European geopolitical developments and broader European literary traditions. The [[Hundred Years' War]] helped foster the development of Portuguese [[chronicle]]s by [[Fernão Lopes]], which constitute a valuable record of some of Europe's early encounters with peoples beyond the continent.{{sfnp|Macedo|2009|pp=2–5}}{{sfnp|Parkinson|2009|pp=45–51}} European medieval chivalric literature, together with didactic religious literature transmitted through adaptations and partial translations, contributed to the development of Portuguese poetry in the translated works of [[Norman French]] [[List of Arthurian literature|Arturian narratives]].{{sfnp|Macedo|2009|p=2}}{{sfnp|Parkinson|2009|pp=53–54}} Portuguese literature flourished during the Age of Discovery with writers such as [[Luís Vaz de Camões]] and [[António Ferreira (poet)|António Ferreira]].{{sfnp|Willis|2009|pp=72–83}}{{sfnp|Perkins|Earle|2009|p=68}} Modern Portuguese literature took shape through the work of [[Almeida Garrett]], one of the founders of Portuguese Romanticism.{{sfnp|Carvalhão Buescu|2009|pp=109–111}} Portugal has one [[Nobel Prize in Literature|Nobel Prize&ndash;winning author]]&mdash;[[José Saramago]] (1998).<ref>{{cite web |title=The Nobel Prize in Literature 1998 - NobelPrize.org |url=https://www.nobelprize.org/prizes/literature/1998/summary/ |access-date=27 December 2025 |publisher=Nobel Prize Outreach |publication-date=2026 |author=<!-- not stated --> |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20260424120342/https://www.nobelprize.org/prizes/literature/1998/summary/ |archive-date=24 April 2026 |url-status=live}}</ref>

=== Music ===
{{Main|Music of Portugal}}

{{CSS image crop
|Alt=Black-and-white photograph of Amália Rodrigues singing into a standing microphone, wearing a long dark gown and shawl, with one hand raised in expression and a guitarist partly visible in the background.
|Image = Amalia Rodrigues, Bestanddeelnr 922-1813 (cropped).jpg
|bSize = 280
|cWidth = 190
|cHeight = 200
|oTop = 40
|oLeft = 45
|Location = right
|Description = [[Amália Rodrigues]] was known as the "Queen of Fado".{{sfnp|Aguiar-Branco|2024|p=1}}
}}

The [[Music history of Portugal|recorded history of music in Portugal]] dates back to the 6th century. The earliest documentation of a church singer in Portugal is from the year 525 and refers to an individual named André. Portuguese music initially consisted mostly of [[liturgical music]] and [[Galician-Portuguese lyric|troubadourism]].{{sfnp|Ferreira|2008|p=10}}{{sfnp|Ferreira|2014|pp=170–178}} Over time, new folk traditions together with the influence of foreign cultures and the creation of [[List of Portuguese musical instruments|new instruments]], especially {{ill|Portuguese guitars|pt|Violas portuguesas|lt=guitars}}, led to a diverse variety of regional folk music such as the fado, the [[Coimbra fado]], and [[Music of Madeira|Madeira's folk music]].{{sfnp|Ribeiro|2026|pp=1–18}}{{sfnp|Lã|Silva|Granqvist|2023|p=631.e7}}{{sfnp|dos Santos|2013|pp=147-153}}

[[List of Portuguese musicians|Popular music]] in Portugal after the Carnation Revolution has been heavily influenced by American trends, which has led into the evolution of [[hip-hop tuga]] and popularization of [[Rock music in Portugal|rock]].{{sfnp|Alge|2013|pp=7–24}} Historically, Portugal has been a country of [[emigration]] which has heavily influenced the [[pimba]] in the 20th century and led to the introduction of Portuguese music into other cultures such as the [[ukelele]] in [[Music of Hawaii|Hawaii]] in the 19th century.{{sfnp|Elliott|2010|p=[https://books.google.com/books?id=Sa-gR-P-pVEC&q=pimba+music&pg=PA135 135]}}{{sfnp|Nidel|2004|p=312}}

=== Holidays and festivities ===
{{Main|Public holidays in Portugal}}

[[File:Entrudo de Vila Boa de Ousilhão 26.jpg|alt=People dressed up as Caretos leap around a bonfire during the Entrudo.|thumb|Two [[Careto]]s at the {{ill|Entrudo|pt|Entrudo|lt=Entrudo}}, in [[Vinhais]]]]

Officially, Portugal has 13 national, government-recognised holidays.{{sfnp|Torgal|2026|pp=1-13}} Public holidays in Portugal are regulated by the {{ill|Portuguese Labour Code|pt|Código do Trabalho|lt=Labour Code}}.{{sfnp|Torgal|2026|pp=1-13}} Besides the national holidays, there are regional holidays celebrated only in Madeira and the Azores and two facultative holidays which are the {{ill|Entrudo|pt|Entrudo|lt=Entrudo}} and one municipal holiday allowed per [[concelho]].{{sfnp|Assembly of the Republic|2009}} Unlike some official holidays, Portugal's folk festivities remain widely attended and are rooted in local traditions, such as the [[Carnival of Ovar]], Lisbon's [[Marchas Populares]], and Porto's [[Festa de São João do Porto|Festa de São João]].{{sfnp|Torgal|2026|pp=1-13}}{{sfnp|Carvalho|Costa|2022|p=73}} Many of folk festivities are associated with the {{lang|pt-PT|[[Folk saint|Santos Populares]]}}, Christian celebrations with older pagan origins linked to [[Midsummer]].{{sfnp|Carvalho|Costa|2022|pp=74-78}} These festivities are celebrated across the country, especially in rural areas, and typically include music performances, stages, temporary arenas, and traditional food stalls.{{sfnp|Carvalho|Costa|2022|pp=79-82}}

=== Cuisine ===
{{Main|Portuguese cuisine|Portuguese wine}}

[[File:Funchal (Madeira, Portugal), Rua Dr. Fernão de Ornelas, Nata 7 - Fernão Ornelas -- 2025 -- 1205.jpg|thumb|{{lang|pt-PT|[[Pastel de nata|Pastéis de nata]]}}|alt=Photograph of a bakery display case filled with pastéis de nata, with a sign in front labelled "Pastel de Nata" and describing them as a low-sugar secret recipe in Portuguese and English.]]

Portuguese cuisine is influenced by both the [[Mediterranean diet|Mediterranean]] and [[Atlantic diet|Atlantic]] diets.{{sfnp|Guiné|Florença|Amaral|Costa|2025|pp=1–2}} Seafood, ''[[brassicas]]'', potatoes, bread, dairy, and [[olive oil]] are traditional staples.{{sfnp|Lorenzo|Izquierdo|Rodriguez-Carnero|Fernández-Pombo|2022|pp=1725–1747}} {{lang|pt-PT|[[Bacalhau]]}} has such a broad presence in Portugal that it is considered a [[national dish]], along with the {{lang|pt|[[pastel de nata]]}}.{{sfnp|Sá|da Silva|2019|pp=4-19}}{{sfnp|Costa e Silva|Elo|2017|pp=425–442}} Traditional Portuguese sweets are known as [[conventual sweets]]. Large quantities of sugar and eggs are used.{{sfnp|Barata Dias|2018|pp=275-299}}

Popular Portuguese beverages include its wines, a craft that is well establish since the 7th century, and of which are such examples as [[Port wine|Port]] and [[Madeira wine|Madeira]].{{sfnp|Cunha|Ibáñez|Teixeira-Santos|Brazão|2020}}{{sfnp|Charzyński|Łyszkiewicz|Musiał|2017|pp=85-100}} [[Beer in Portugal|Beer]] has been brewed in Portugal since the [[Chalcolithic Europe|Chalcolithic]].{{sfnp|Estreicher|2025|p=40}} Tea has been produced on São Miguel Island since the 19th century.{{sfnp|Moura|2019|pp=112-130}}

=== Sport ===
{{Main|Sport in Portugal}}

{{CSS image crop
|Image =Cristiano Ronaldo Portugal.jpg
|alt =Cristiano Ronaldo playing for Portugal during a football match.
|bSize = 190
|cWidth = 190
|cHeight = 220
|oTop = 0
|oLeft = 0
|Location = left
|Description = [[Cristiano Ronaldo]] is widely considered one of the [[List of career achievements by Cristiano Ronaldo|greatest football players in history]].
}}

Portugal has contributed to the popularity and globalisation of sport through internationally recognised athletes such as [[Cristiano Ronaldo]], [[Ricardinho (futsal player, born 1985)|Ricardinho]], [[Naide Gomes]], and [[Carlos Lopes]], as well as through the [[List of sports clubs by membership|popularity]] of its three largest [[Big Three (Portugal)|football clubs]].{{sfnp|García-Fernández|Grimaldi-Puyana|Bravo|2022|p=2}}{{sfnp|Carvalho|García-Cirac|Sousa|2022|p=36}} [[Football in Portugal|Football]] is the most popular sport in Portugal.{{sfnp|Almeida|2022|pp=75–88}} The [[Portugal men's national football team]] won the [[UEFA European Championship]] in [[UEFA Euro 2016|2016]] and the [[UEFA Nations League]] in [[2018–19 UEFA Nations League|2019]] and [[2024–25 UEFA Nations League|2025]].{{sfnp|''Full time report final - Sunday 10 July 2016 Stade de France - Saint-Denis''|2016}}{{sfnp|''Full time report final - Sunday 9 June 2019 - Estádio do Dragão - Porto''|2019}}{{sfnp|''Full time report final - Sunday 8 June 2025 - Munich Football Arena''|2025}}

Portugal is among the world's leading [[Futsal in Portugal|futsal]] countries,<ref>{{cite web |url=https://inside.fifa.com/fifa-rankings |publisher=FIFA |author=<!-- not stated --> |access-date=11 May 2026 |date=8 May 2026 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20260511052247/https://inside.fifa.com/fifa-rankings |archive-date=11 May 2026 |url-status=live |title=FIFA rankings}}</ref> with its [[Portugal national futsal team|men's national team]] having won the [[FIFA Futsal World Cup]] in [[2021 FIFA Futsal World Cup|2021]],<ref>{{cite web |url=https://www.uefa.com/futsalworldcup/news/0257-0de805f9ebe1-fdf0b055945a-1000--2021-fifa-futsal-world-cup-portugal-beat-argentina-in-final/ |title=2021 FIFA Futsal World Cup: Portugal beat Argentina in final |publisher=FIFA |author=<!-- not stated --> |date=30 September 2021 |access-date=11 May 2026 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20211022183728/https://www.uefa.com/futsalworldcup/news/0257-0de805f9ebe1-fdf0b055945a-1000--2021-fifa-futsal-world-cup-portugal-beat-argentina-in-final/ |archive-date=22 October 2021 |url-status=live}}</ref> the [[UEFA Futsal Championship]] in [[UEFA Futsal Euro 2018|2018]] and [[UEFA Futsal Euro 2022|2022]],{{sfnp|''UEFA Futsal EURO 2018 tournament review''|2018|p=23}}{{sfnp|''UEFA Futsal EURO 2022 tournament review''|2022|p=27}} and the [[Futsal Finalissima]] in [[2022 Futsal Finalissima|2022]].<ref>{{cite web |url=https://www.uefa.com/futsalfinalissima/match/2036154--spain-vs-portugal/ |title=Spain-Portugal |publisher=UEFA |author=<!-- not stated --> |date=18 September 2022 |access-date=11 May 2026 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20260511063133/https://www.uefa.com/futsalfinalissima/match/2036154--spain-vs-portugal/ |archive-date=11 May 2026 |url-status=live}}</ref> In {{ill|Sport of athletics in Portugal|pt|Atletismo em Portugal|lt=athletics}}, the country has set several [[List of Portuguese records in athletics|records]] and has earned more medals in this sport than in any other at both the [[Portugal at the Olympics|Olympic]] and [[Portugal at the Paralympics|Paralympic Games]].{{sfnp|''2024 activities and accounts report''|2025|pp=54–95}}<ref>{{cite web |url=https://comiteolimpicoportugal.pt/atletas/atletas-olimpicos/resultados/ |title=Resultados |language=pt-PT |trans-title=Results |publisher=Comité Olímpico de Portugal |author-link=Olympic Committee of Portugal |publication-date=1 November 2024 |author=<!-- not stated --> |access-date=11 May 2026}}</ref>{{sfnp|''Paralympic medals''|2026|pp=2-9}} In traditional sport, Portugal is noted for its [[Portuguese School of Equestrian Art|classical dressage]] as well as native sports such as [[jogo do pau]] and {{ill|Jogo da malha|pt|Jogo da malha|lt=jogo da malha}}.{{sfnp|Pickel-Chevalier|2020|pp=22–44}}{{sfnp|Ramírez-Macías|Rodríguez-Sánchez|Lasaga-Rodríguez|2022|pp=199–208}} Portugal has several established sporting centres across the country, including the Algarve and Lisbon, which are international golf destinations and have hosted motorsport events such as [[Portuguese Grand Prix|Formula One]] and [[Portuguese motorcycle Grand Prix|Grand Prix motorcycle racing]],{{sfnp|Moreira|2018}}{{sfnp|Castro|Menezes|2025|pp=29–47}}{{sfnp|Mourão|Macedo|Sánchez-Carreira|2025|p=198}} as well as [[Praia do Norte (Nazaré)|Nazaré]] and [[Supertubos|Peniche]], which are known for surfing and annually host the {{ill|TUDOR Nazaré Big Wave Challenge|es|Big Wave World Tour}} and the [[MEO Rip Curl Pro Portugal]], respectively.{{sfnp|Fernandes|Ribeiro|Ferreira|2017|pp=703–712}}<ref>{{cite web |url=https://www.worldsurfleague.com/events/2025/bwt/472/tudor-nazar-big-wave-challenge/results |title=Results - TUDOR Nazaré Big Wave Challenge 2025 |publisher=World Surf League |author=<!-- not stated --> |publication-date=31 March 2026 |access-date=11 May 2026 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20260511161329/https://www.worldsurfleague.com/events/2025/bwt/472/tudor-nazar-big-wave-challenge/results |archive-date=11 May 2026 |url-status=live}}</ref><ref>{{cite web |url=https://www.worldsurfleague.com/events/2026/ct/445/meo-rip-curl-pro-portugal |publication-date=4 February 2026 |title=Main - MEO Rip Curl Pro Portugal 2026 |publisher=World Surf League |author=<!-- not stated --> |access-date=11 May 2026
